The Race and Ethnicity Committee conducted focus groups with attorneys and with employees (and benefitted from some additional focus groups conducted by the Gender Committee). In addition to the many Committee members who acted as facilitators for these groups, the Committee wants to thank those outside the Committee who volunteered their time to facilitate focus groups, including Phillip Lattimore, Maggie Prieto, Poli Marmolejos, George Kassouf, Wayne Williams, Theodore Marcus, Kenneth Marks, Gilberto deJesus, and 

William Ho-Gonzalez.

The Gender Committee conducted a total of 29 focus groups with attorneys and with employees (and benefitted from some additional focus groups conducted by the Race and Ethnicity Committee). In addition to the many Committee members who acted as facilitators for these groups (including all co-chairs, and members Anita Barondes, Joel P. Bennett, Jane 

Golden Belford, Michele Ellison, Robert Kopp, Stephen J. Pollak, Jack Scheuermann and

Judith Winston) the Committee wants to thank those outside the Committee who volunteered their time to facilitate focus groups, including Carolyn Lerner, Stephanie Wickouski, Mark 

Foster, Katharyn Marks, Catherine Crystal, Penny Marshall, and Jane Macht.

JOINT APPENDICES APPENDIX B THE EMPLOYEE SURVEY* 

*The employee survey materials are referred to in the Gender Report as Appendix 

E and in the Race and Ethnicity Report as Appendix H.

MEMORANDUM To: 

From: 

SAR

Re: 

Date: 

All Court Employees Prof. Susan Deller Ross, CoChair, Special Committee on Gender 

Loseph Sellers, Esq., CoChair, Special Committee on Race & Ethnicity 

Katherine L. Garrett, Executive Director 

Employee Questionnaire 

February 1994

The D.C. Circuit Judicial Council established the Task Force on Gender, Race and Ethnic Bias to study whether and to what extent gender, race, and ethnicity affect the operations and proceedings of the federal courts of the District of Columbia Circuit. 

The D.C. Circuit is one of the first federal circuits in the nation to examine these important issues, and the first to look at how these issues affect the worklife of court employees. The Task Force has appointed leading academics and members of the bar to the Special Committee on Gender and the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity to carry out its mandate. The preliminary findings of the Special Committees are to be presented at the June 1994 D.C. Circuit Judicial Conference, and reported to the Circuit 

Judicial Council for appropriate action.

Many of you have participated in the confidential employee focus groups, interviews, and roundtables held by the Special Committees. Information from those discussions has helped direct the Special Committees' exploration of worklife in the Courthouse. To further understand issues within the Courthouse, the Special 

Committees have developed the enclosed confidential questionnaire of employees' experiences and observations. This questionnaire is a significant part of the effort to study issues of gender, race, and ethnicity in the Courthouse. We are asking each employee to participate in the survey to ensure that the information gathered will accurately reflect the diversity of jobs, experiences, and backgrounds found within the Courthouse. To ensure that the study accurately reflects the views and experiences of a cross-section of Court employees, it is important that a large response be received. We need your response!

Please take a few minutes to complete the enclosed questionnaire and return it in the envelope provided at your earliest convenience, and no later than March 4, 1994. If you have additional views you would like to share with the Special Committees, please note them in the margins or on additional pages. All of the questionnaires, and the answers you provide, will be treated as confidential. No information that might identify individual respondents will be reported. The results will be tabulated by social scientists at American University and the University of Delaware, and reported as statistical frequencies and averages. Your name will not be placed on the questionnaire or any reports.

We do recognize how valuable your time is and how valuable your response will be, and are most appreciative of your assistance in this study. If you have any questions, please contact Katia Garrett at 273-0477. Thanks!

Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY 

Employee Survey 

.C. Circuit Task Force ca Gender, Race, and Ethnic Blas - United States Courthouse Washington, DC 20001

QUESTIONNAIRE FOR COURT EMPLOYEES 

This questionnaire has been designed to be easy and quick to complete. Most of the questions ask you to circle one or more responses or to provide a small amount of Information. We recognize, however, that many of the issues addressed here are quite complicated. We also recognize that some of the items may not be appropriate for your particular position. 

Consequently, we strongly encourage you to write any comments, explanations, and other relevant information in the margins and on additional pages. 

All of the responses to the questionnaire will be treated as confidential and no Information that might Identify Individual respondents will be reported.

3 5% 1% 1 2% specify: 

Employee Survey Question 3a asked respondents to identify their race, giving them the choice of "African-American/Black; Caucasian/White; Asian-American/Pacific Islander; Native American; Other, Three people completed the "Other" category with the term "Hispanic," 1 with "Eurasian," and 1 with "Human Race." In addition, four people did not answer the race question. The computer analysis by gender and race/ethnicity combined, used throughout this Appendix, counted as "Majority" the "Caucasian/White" response, and as "Minority" (here, "Non-white") the responses of "African-American/Black; Asian 

American/Pacific Islander, Native American; Other (Hispanic); and Other (Eurasian)." It did not include the remaining "Other" response or the 4 who did not answer the question. The data reported in this Appendix are based on a combined "gender/race/ethnicity" analysis of the 238 surveys from respondents who provided both their gender and race (including the "Other" responses of "Hispanic" and "Eurasian," but not "Human Race"); it does not report on the total of 243 surveys the Committee received since 5 of those surveys did not allow identification of the combined gender and race/ethnicity of the respondent. 

Throughout this Appendix, where employees did not answer a question or answered "no experience" or *no opportunity to observe," their responses for the particular question are not reported or included in the calculation of percentages for that question. Percentages may not add to 100% because of rounding; anything at .5 or higher was rounded to the next highest number.

APPENDICES TO THE FINAL REPORT OF THE SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX A ACKNOWLEDGMENTS* 

*The acknowledgments are printed in the Joint Appendices above.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX B PUBLIC DOCUMENTS CONCERNING THE TASK FORCE

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX B.1 TASK FORCE/SPECIAL COMMITTEE BACKGROUND

April 6, 1993

I. Mandate and Scope The District of Columbia Circuit Task Force on Gender, Race and Ethnic Bias was established in 1990 by the D.C. Circuit Judicial Council, under the leadership of then Chief 

Judge Patricia Wald. The Task Force has a broad mandate to examine whether and how gender and race affect the work and worklife of the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals and of the United States District Court for the District of Coumbia. The results of the Task 

Force's review and its recommendations will be reported to the Judicial Council for appropriate action.

Together with the Ninth Circuit, which recently completed a preliminary report on gender bias and is considering undertaking a similar study of race and ethnic bias, the D.C. 

Circuit has taken a leadership role among the federal courts in investigating gender, race and ethnic bias so as to ensure the fair and equal treatment of all individuals who come before its courts, whether as employees, litigants, lawyers, jurors, witnesses or otherwise. 

Some thirty-five state court systems, including the District of Colulmbia, have undertaken or completed examinations of gender bias. Far fewer have thus far studied the effects of race, or ethnicity in the court systems. The D.C. Circuit Task Force is now undertaking comprehensive studies of gender, race and ethnic bias. 

The Task Force is made up of four judges, appointed by the Judicial Council. The 

Chair is United States District Judge Joyce Hens Green. The other members are Judge Ruth Bader Ginsburg, of the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals; Judge John Garrett Penn, Chief 

Judge of the United States District Court; and United States District Judge Charles R. Richey.

II. Task Force Structure 

90 The Task Force has created two Special Committees -- one on Gender Bias and one on Race and Ethnic Bias. Each Special Committee is co-chaired by leading attorneys appointed by the judicial Task Force. The co-chairs of the Special Committee on Gender are Professors Vicki C. Jackson and Susan Deller Ross, both of Georgetown University Law 

Center, and Susan M. Liss, Esq., Director/Counsel, Citizens' Conmission on Civil Rights.

The co-chairs of the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity are Vincent H. Cohen, a partner with Hogan & Hartson, Professor Todd D. Peterson of George Washington 

University National Law Center, and Vanessa Ruiz, Esq., Deputy Corporation Counsel with the D.C. Office of Corporation Counsel. 

Each Special Committee will include as additional members academics, litigators and other practitioners with expertise in fields relevant to the work of the Task Force. Social scientists will advise each Special Committee in its work. The Task Force has one full-time staff member, Executive Director Katherine L. 

Garrett, Esq. She reports directly to the Chair of the Task Force and works with the 

Special Committees' Co-Chairs, coordinating and facilitating their work.

III. Research 

Each Special Committee identifies the substantive areas and issues appropriate for study. The research is then carried out by substantive subcommittees. The Special 

Committee on Race & Ethnicity will be holding a public hearing in early May to identify the issues of greatest concern to the affected communities, and will develop its research agenda from there.

The Special Committee on Gender, which began its work in the summer of 1992, has identified several substantive areas for study, and has established seven subcommittees to help carry out its research: 1) demographics, 2) employee worklife, 3) litigation process, 4) federal equality law, 5) criminal justice, 6) administrative law, and 7) race, class and gender. As work progresses, the kinds and numbers of subcommittees may change to meet evolving needs. Several subcommittees have begun holding initial informal focus groups to narrow the issues being investigated and to gather anecdotal information about the role of gender in the courts' processes. 1 Both Special Committees are discussing a variety of means for gathering data, including surveys of litigators, jurors, and employees, interviews with court personnel, focus groups, and review and analysis of existing demographic and statistical information available through the courts' computerized dockets, the Administrative Office, and other court-related entities (e.g., U.S. Sentencing Commission, Federal Judicial Center, etc.) 

The target date for completion of a preliminary report is the June 1994 Judicial Conference. [This document was updated periodically and made available to interested parties by the Task Force's Executive Director.] 1 Although the Special Committee on Race & Ethnicity is investigating race and ethnicity issues, the Special Committee on Gender established this subcommittee to assure that it is attentive to the interesecting effects of race and class on issues of gender bias.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX B.2 U.S. JUDICIAL CONFERENCE RESOLUTIONS

Report of the 

Proceedings of the Judicial Conference of the

United States 

September 22, 1992

Washington, D. C. 

1992

CERTIFICATION OF INTERPRETERS Upon request of the Judicial Councils of the Seventh and Ninth Circuits for certification of interpreters in 12 additional languages, the Committee on Court Administration and Case Management reviewed an analysis of the Interpreting needs of these circuits and examined a proposal by the University of Arizona for the development of a more cost effective method for certifying and qualifying interpreters. Pending the availability of funds, the Judicial 

Conference approved the Committee's recommendation that the Conference direct the Administrative Office to provide for full certification of interpreters in Cantonese (Chinese), Ilocano, Korean, Mandarin (Chinese), Punjabi, Tagalog and Vietnamese; for modified certification of interpreters in Armenian, Japanese, Laotian and Polish; and for "Otherwise Qualified" status in Mein.

BIAS IN THE FEDERAL JUDICIARY 

Concluding that bias, in all of its forms, presents a danger to the effective administration of justice in federal courts, the Committee recommended, and the Judicial Conference adopted, a resolution encouraging each circuit not already doing so to sponsor educational programs for judges, supporting personnel and attorneys to sensitize them to concerns of bias based on race, ethnicity, gender, age and disability, and the extent to which bias may affect litigants, witnesses, attorneys and all those who work in the judicial branch. 

Further, the Judicial Conference voted to encourage each circuit to report to the Committee on Court Administration and Case Management on action taken to implement this resolution.

MISCELLANEOUS FEES 

Although the Judicial Conference schedules of fees for the appellate, district and bankruptcy courts provide for a handling fee of $25 for a check paid into the court and returned for lack of funds (28 U.S.C. §§ 1913, 1914), a comparable provision was not included in the schedule of fees for the United 

States Claims Court (28 U.S.C. § 1927). Because the rationale for assessing such a fee exists equally in the Claims Court, the Conference approved a Committee recommendation to amend the schedule of fees for the United States Claims Court to include a $25 fee for checks paid into the Court which are returned for lack of funds.

In order to recover more effectively the full costs of noticing in the bankruptcy courts, the Judicial Conference approved the collection of a miscellaneous administrative fee of $30 in all chapter 7 and chapter 13 cases, in lieu of noticing fees currently charged by the clerk of court under 28 U.S.C. § 1930(b). In addition, the Conference directed the Committee on the 64

EXCERPT FROM THE REPORT OF THE PROCEEDINGS OF THE JUDICIAL CONFERENCE OF THE UNITED STATES MARCH 16, 1993 WASHINGTON, D.C. 1993 AD HOC COMMITTEE ON GENDER-BASED VIOLENCE The Judicial Conference adopted the following resolution: 

After considering the report and recommendations of the Ad Hoc Committee on Gender-Based Violence, and as a result of the dialogue the Ad Hoc Committee has undertaken with the sponsors of the proposed Violence Against Women Act of 1991 (S. 15, 102d

Congress) since the Judicial Conference adopted its resolution about the proposed 1991 Act (JCUS-SEP 91, pp 57-58), the Conference takes no position on specific provisions of the proposed 

Violence Against Women Act of 1993 (S.11, 103d Congress). The 

Conference reiterates its concerns expressed in its prior resolution and its general concerns about the trend toward federalization of state law crimes and causes of action.

At the same time, the Conference believes that the provision of Title V of S. 11, encouraging circuit judicial councils to conduct studies with respect to gender bias in their respective circuits, has great merit and the Conference endorses that specific provision.

CONGRESSIONAL RECORD-SENATE 

Mr. DOLE. Mr. President, I am pleased to be an original cosponsor of the Flexible Family Leave Tax Credit 

Act of 1993, which provides for refundable tax credits for businesses that establish nondiscriminatory parental leave policies.

FLEXIBLE FAMILY LEAVE TAX CREDIT ACT OF 

1993 

This credit would be available for all businesses with under 500 employeescovering 6 million businesses and almost 50 million workers. 

The amount of the credit would be for 20 percent of total employee compensation of up to $2,000 per month for a period of up to 12 weeks. In other words, the credit would amount to $100/ week or a maximum of $1,200 per employee to cover agreed-upon benefits during the period of absence. 

An employee would be eligible to take leave in the event of the birth of a child, the placement of a child with the employee for adoption or foster care, or for a child, parent, or spouse with a serious health condition, or a serious health condition that prevents the employee from performing his or her job.

ADVANTAGES OF LEGISLATION 

Mr. President, the need to enact family leave legislation is greater than ever. We have more and more households where both parents are in the workplace and more and more households run by single parents. We are told that these trends are here to stay. nesses to establish family and medical leave programs. 

Democrat alternatives, on the other hand, are mandates; hidden taxes; 

Washington, DC reaching out into every community, every office, and every factory telling the American people what is best for them.

It is an approach that for all its good intentions to help families will ultimately cause more harm than good.

PRO-JOBS 

Indeed, legislation mandating benefits demands an offset and will force employers to cut jobs or other more desirable employee benefits to pay for the hidden tax.

Some cost estimates run as high as $7 billion for such legislation. 

To say the least, that's a lot of jobsparticularly at a time when we have an unemployment rate that is unacceptably high.

The legislation offered by the distinguished Senator from Idaho is a projobs bill. Not only does it not tax employers to pay for a congressionally mandated benefit, but the credit provides an incentive to create new jobs to temporarily fill the places of those workers on leave. 

You hear a lot of speeches around this place and in the media about creating jobs-preserving jobs. 

And yet, anyone who supports legislation mandating that employers provide this benefit are doing the exact opposite. Simply put, mandating fam 

The key for me is to find a legislatively leave will force employers to mandate a permanent leave program for solution to this issue that is both prosome of their other workers. family and pro-business. I believe that the legislation introduced by the distinguished Senator from Idaho meets these two important objectives.

PRO-FAMILY 

It is pro-family because it provides for a flexible leave program that allows the employer and employee-and not the Federal Government-to freely determine the terms of leave that works best for them.

It provides for the same types of leave and the same types of protections as legislation supported by my colleagues on the other side of the aisle. 

And this it accomplishes without all of the harmful effects that mandates cause to the economy and to efforts to create new jobs.

Finally, it is worth noting that the legislation offered by my distinguished colleague helps 15 million more families than legislation promoted by the other side of the aisle which excludes businesses with under 50 employees in a weak attempt to limit the acknowledged economic damage such legislation would do to smaller businesses. 

PRO-BUSINESS 

The legislation introduced by my distinguished colleague from Idaho is also a pro-business package and stands in sharp contrast to legislation supported by my colleagues on the other side of the aisle.

The Flexible Family Leave Tax Cred it Act provides an incentive for busi- 

DEFICIT NEUTRAL 

The legislation introduced by the distinguished Senator from Idaho is also deficit neutral. It contains a pay-for previously contained in H.R. 11 from the 102d Congress which increases the corporate estimated tax to 100 percent.

I think everyone will agree that it is rather than do so, my colleagues on the not easy to pay for legislation. Indeed, other side of the aisle prefer to impose mandates on business so that they have to pay for it.

In this Senator's opinion, that is not the direction we should be moving in.

It hurts the economy and it hurts jobs. It is the easy way out an out of sight-out of mind approach that shirks the responsibility we have to pay for the programs we make into laws. 

JUDGE LEGISLATION BY MERIT-NOT BY POLITICS 

Mr. President, we all support family and medical leave. There has never been a debate on the need and value of such programs. Rather, the debate has always been how you go about doing it, and it is there that the difference of opinion could not be greater. 

I urge my colleagues to carefully review this legislation based on its merits not on the politics that has governed this issue for the last 7 years. 

My colleagues on the other side of the aisle support a bill that is an in kind tax on business-a clumsy, harmful one-size-fits-all mandate.

I think we can do better and the distinguished Senator from Idaho has done just that in the legislation he has introduced today.

S. 11. A bill to combat violence and crimes against women on the streets and in homes; to the Committee on the Judiciary.

VIOLENCE AGAINST WOMEN ACT 

Mr. BIDEN. Mr. President, I rise today to introduce Senate bill number 

11, the Violence Against Women Act of 

1993-the first comprehensive legislation to address the growing problem of violent crime confronting American women.

Since I first introduced this legislation in 1990, the Judiciary Committee has held a series of four hearings; we have refined the legislation and issued reports; we have garnered the support of prominent groups and individuals with widely differing interests-from law enforcement, women's groups, and victims' advocates. The bill has twice received the unanimous approval of the 

Judiciary Committee. Now, it is time to complete our efforts. 

I am particularly heartened that my colleague, the gentlewoman from Callfornia [Mrs. BOXER], the author of this measure in the House of Representatives can join me this year, here in the Senate, to help fight for this legislation. I know that she shares my conviction that we must do everything in our power to ensure that the Violence

Against Women Act becomes law this year. 

We have waited in my view too long, already, to recognize the horror and the sweep of this violence. For too many years, our idea of crime has left no room for violence against women. 

We now face a problem that has become doubly dangerous, as invisible to policymakers as it is terrifying to its victims. 

Our blindness costs us dearly: 

Every week, 21,000 women report to police that they been beaten in their own homes; 

Every day, over 2,500 women visit an emergency room because of a violent act perpetrated against their persons;

SEC. 613 COOPERATION IN DEVELOPING PRO- GRAMS IN MAKING GRANTS UNDER THIS TITLE The State Justice Institute shall ensure that model programs carried out pursuant to grants made under this subtitle are developed with the participation of law enforcement officials, public and private nonprofit victim advocates, legal experts, prosecutors, defense attorneys, and recognized experts on gender bias in the courts.

BEC. 514. AUTHORIZATION OF APPROPRIATIONS. 

There is authorized to be appropriated to carry out this subtitle $600,000 for fiscal year 1993. Of amounts appropriated under this section, the State Justice Institute shall expend no less than 40 percent on model programs regarding domestic violence and no less than 

40 percent on model programs regarding rape and sexual assault. 

Subtitle B-Education and Training for Judges and Court Personnel in Federal 

Courts

BEC. 1. AUTHORIZATIONS OF CIRCUIT STUDIES, EDUCATION AND TRAINING GRANTS 

(a) STUDY.-In order to gain a better understanding of the nature and the extent of gender bias in the Federal courts, the circuit judicial councils are encouraged to conduct studies of the instances, if any, of gender bias in their respective circuits. The studies may include an examination of the effects of gender on- (1) the treatment of litigants, witnesses, attorneys, jurors, and judges in the courts, including before magistrate and bankruptcy judges; (2) the interpretation and application of the law, both civil and criminal; (3) treatment of defendants in criminal cases; (4) treatment of victims of violent crimes; (5) sentencing: (6) sentencing alternatives, facilities for incarceration, and the nature of supervision of probation and parole; (7) appointments to committees of the Judicial Conference and the courts; (8) case management and court sponsored alternative dispute resolution programs; (9) the selection, retention, promotion, and treatment of employees; (10) appointment of arbitrators, experts, and special masters; and (11) the aspects of the topics listed in section 512 that pertain to issues within the jurisdiction of the Federal courts.

Judicial Con- (b) CLEARINGHOUSE.—The ference of the United States shall designate an entity within the Judicial branch to act as a clearinghouse to disseminate any reports and materials issued by the gender bias task forces under subsection (a) and to respond to requests for such reports and materials. The gender bias task forces shall provide this entity with their reports and related material. 

(c) MODEL PROGRAMS.-The Federal Judi cial Center, in carrying out section 620xbx3) 

of title 28, United States Code, may- 

(1) include in the educational programs it presents and prepares, including the training programs for newly appointed judges, information on issues related to gender bias in the courts including such areas as are listed in subsection (a) along with such other topics as the Federal Judicial Center deems appropriate; (2) prepare materials necessary to implement this subsection; and (3) take into consideration the findings and recommendations of the studies conducted pursuant to subsection (a), and to consult with individuals and groups with relevant expertise in gender bias issues as it prepares or revises such materials.

BEC. 122. AUTHORIZATION OF APPROPRIATIONS 

(a) IN GENERAL.

-There is authorized to be appropriated- 

(1) $400,000 to the Salaries and Expenses Account of the Courts of Appeals, District 

Courts, and other Judicial Services, to carry out section 521(a), to be available until expended through fiscal year 1994; (2) $100,000 to the Federal Judicial Center to carry out section 521(c) and any activities designated by the Judicial Conference under section 521(b); and (3) such sums as are necessary to the Ad ministrative Office of the United States 

Courts to carry out any activities designated by the Judicial Conference under section 

521(b). 

(b) THE JUDICIAL CONFERENCE OF THE UNIT- ED STATES. (1) The Judicial Conference of the United States Courts shall allocate funds to Federal circuit courts under this subtitle that or 8357 (A) undertake studies in their own circuits; (B) implement reforms recommended as a result of such studies in their own or other circuits, including education and training. (2) Funds shall be allocated to Federal circuits under this subtitle on a first come first serve basis in an amount not to exceed $50,000 on the first application. If within 6 months after the date on which funds anthorised under this Act become available, funds are still available, circuits that have received funds may reapply for additional funds, with not more than $200,000 going to any one circuit.

BIDEN VIOLENCE AGAINST WOMEN ACT OF 1983 

(8.11) 

TITLE I-SAFE STREETS FOR WOMEN 

Creates new penalties for sex crimes 

Creates new penalties for sex offenders. 

Increases restitution for the victims of sex crimes.

Encourages women to prosecute their attackers 

Extends "rape shield law" protection to civil cases (e.g. sexual harassment cases) and all criminal cases (other than sexual assault cases where it already applies) to bar embarrassing and irrelevant inquiries into a victim's sexual history at trial.

Bars the use of a woman's clothing to show, at trial, that the victim incited or invited a sexual assault. 

Requires States to pay to rape exams. 

Targets places most dangerous for women, including public transit and parks 

Authorizes $300 million for law enforcement efforts to combat violence against women, aiding police, prosecutors and victim advocates.

Funds increased lighting and camera surveillance at bus stops, bus stations, subways and parking lots and targets existing funds for increased lighting, emergency telephones and police in public parks.

Education and prevention 

Authorizes $65 million for rape education, starting in junior high. 

Establishes the "National Commission on

Violent Crime Against Women"' 

Creates a commission to develop a national strategy for combating violence against women.

TITLE -SAFE HOMES FOR WOMEN 

Protects women from abusive spouses 

Creates federal penalties for spouse abusers who cross state lines to continue their abuse. 

Requires all states to enforce any "stayaway" order, regardless of which state issues it.

Promotes arrests of abusive spouses Authorizes $25 million for states that promote the arrest of abusing spouses and experiment with new techniques to increase prosecution. 

Provides more money for shelters 

Boosts funding for battered women's shelters. 

Establishes a national domestic violence hotline

Provides federal funding for a national domestic violence hotline (Senator Kennedy). 

Increases research and data 

Author zes research and increases data collection on violence against women. 

TITLE II-CIVIL RIGHTS FOR WOMEN 

Brtends "Civil Rights" protections to all gender-motivated crimes

Makes gender-based assaults a violation of federal civil rights laws. 

Allows victims of all felonies "motivated by gender" to bring civil rights suits against their assailants.

CHAPTER 2-EDUCATION AND TRAINING FOR JUDGES AND COURT PERSONNEL IN FEDERAL COURTS SEC. 40421. AUTHORIZATIONS OF CIRCUIT STUDIES; EDUCATION AND TRAINING GRANTS. 

(a) STUDIES.-In order to gain a better understanding of the nature and the extent of gender bias in the Federal courts, the circuit judicial councils are encouraged to conduct studies of the instances, if any, of gender bias in their respective circuits and to implement recommended reforms. 

(b) MATTERS FOR EXAMINATION.-The studies under subsection 

(a) may include an examination of the effects of gender on- 

(1) the treatment of litigants, witnesses, attorneys, jurors, and judges in the courts, including before magistrate and bankruptcy judges; (2) the interpretation and application of the law, both civil and criminal; (3) treatment of defendants in criminal cases; (4) treatment of victims of violent crimes in judicial proceedings; (5) sentencing; (6) sentencing alternatives and the nature of supervision of probation and parole; (7) appointments to committees of the Judicial Conference and the courts; (8) case management and couri sponsored alternative dispute resolution programs; (9) the selection, retention, promotion, and treatment of employees;

PUBLIC LAW 103-322-SEPT. 13, 1994 

(10) appointment of arbitrators, experts, and special masters; (11) the admissibility of the victim's past sexual history in civil and criminal cases; and (12) the aspects of the topics listed in section 40412 that pertain to issues within the jurisdiction of the Federal courts. 

(c) CLEARINGHOUSE.-The Administrative Office of the United 

States Courts shall act as a clearinghouse to disseminate any reports and materials issued by the gender bias task forces under subsection (a) and to respond to requests for such reports and materials. The gender bias task forces shall provide the Administrative Office of the Courts of the United States with their reports and related material. 

(d) MODEL PROGRAMS.-The Federal Judicial Center, in carry: 

ing out section 620(bX3) of title 28, United States Code, may- (1) include in the educational programs it presents and prepares, including the training programs for newly appointed judges, information on issues related to gender bias in the courts including such areas as are listed in subsection (a) along with such other topics as the Federal Judicial Center deems appropriate;. (2) prepare materials necessary to implement this subsection; and (3) take into consideration the findings and recommendations of the studies conducted pursuant to subsection (a), and to consult with individuals and groups with relevant expertise in gender bias issues as it prepares or revises such materials. 

SEC. 40422. AUTHORIZATION OF APPROPRIATIONS. 

There are authorized to be appropriated- 

(1) to the Salaries and Expenses Account of the Courts of Appeals, District Courts, and other Judicial Services to carry out section 40421(a) $500,000 for fiscal year 1996; (2) to the Federal Judicial Center to carry out section 40421(d) $100,000 for fiscal year 1996; and (3) to the Administrative Office of the United States Courts to carry out section 40421(c) $100,000 for fiscal year 1996.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX B.4 CONFERENCE OF STATE CHIEF JUSTICES RESOLUTIONS

RESOLUTIONS ON THE STUDY OF BIAS IN THE COURTS NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the Conference of Chief Justices urges positive action by every chief justice to address gender bias and minority concerns in the state courts; and

BE IT THEREFORE RESOLVED that the Conference of Chief 

Justices urges each chief justice in every state to establish separate task forces devoted to the study of 1) gender bias in the court system and 2) minority concerns as they relate to the judicial system.

This resolution was adopted at the 40th Annual Meeting on August 4, 1988. 

The Conference of Chief Justices continues to encourage state courts to address issues of gender bias and minority concerns. In 1993 the Conference adopted the following resolutions:

Resolution Urging Further Efforts for Equal Treatment of All Persons 

(1993)¹'

WHEREAS, the Conference of Chief Justices has established a 

Committee on Discrimination in the Courts because the principle of equal treatment of all persons before the law is essential to the very concept of justice; and 

WHEREAS, there are a variety of federal, state and local laws and policies regarding all persons and institutions, both public and private, to prevent discrimination against any persons on the bias of race, gender, color, national origin, religion, age or disability, and in some states sexual orientation, and to take Conference of Chief Justices, Resolution Urging Further Efforts for Equal Treatment of All Persons, adopted January 28, 1993 (on file with the Office of the Circuit Executive).

affirmative steps to overcome the effects of discrimination on such grounds; and

WHEREAS, the state courts have been instrumental in enforcing such laws and policies as they apply to other private and public parties in cases coming before the courts; and 

WHEREAS, the Conference of Chief Justices is concerned that all participants in the judicial system are treated fairly and that the judicial system operates free of discrimination against any person; and

WHEREAS, Resolution XVIII adopted by the Conference of 

Chief Justices in 1988 urges positive action by every chief justice to address gender bias and minority concerns in the state courts, and further urges each chief justice in every state to establish separate task forces devoted to the study of (1) gender bias in the court system and (2) minority concerns as they relate to the judicial system.

NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that the Conference of Chief Justices urges each chief justice in every state to further the efforts for equal justice, as defined in the clauses above, in the court system by establishing task forces to remedy any discrimination and to implement the recommendations of the task force studies.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX C TECHNICAL APPENDICES

TECHNICAL APPENDICES FOR THE FINAL REPORT 

OF THE SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER I. Overall Research Tools Utilized By the Committee 

As indicated in the Report, this Committee used a variety of research tools to assist in its work. Some of the research was done by Committee members or advisors, or other volunteers working under their supervision, and some was coordinated through the Executive Director's office. 

In some areas, as well, this Committee was able to use research done by or for the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity. 

A.

The Attorney Survey 

Surveying groups through standardized written questionnaires is a useful way of measuring perceptions and the reported frequency of experiences. Written questionnaires are also useful for collecting demographic data to provide an understanding of a population as a whole. Like any research tool, written questionnaires also have limitations, being based on self-reported data, on the perceptions of the respondents, and on the particular questions asked and response options offered. In the spring and summer of 1993, the Special Committee on Gender designed and administered a written questionnaire to attorneys who had recently litigated in the federal courts of the D.C. Circuit. 1. 

Questionnaire Design

The questionnaire was collectively developed by the members and the social science research directors of the Special Committee on Gender. We relied in part on the questionnaire used in the Ninth Circuit study of gender bias in the federal courts, using some of the same questions to permit cross-circuit comparisons. We also drew on questionnaires from many state court studies. 

Finally, focus groups and the personal insights of Special Committee on Gender members were used to generate additional questions. The questionnaire was pretested by a small group of attorneys and was revised and improved a number of times. 2. 

Selection of the Sample 

The attorney questionnaire was designed for attorneys actively litigating in the D.C. 

Circuit federal courts. Several options for selecting a population of these attorneys were considered. 

The membership lists of the district and appellate courts were not appropriate. The Court of Appeals has a membership list that includes all attorneys who have been admitted since roughly 1960, approximately 30,000 attorneys. The District Court membership is more current, since attorneys are required to renew their membership periodically and to indicate whether they are active. But both Information for this part of the Technical Appendix was derived from a 11/7/93 memo from Katherine Garrett ("Attorney questionnaire sample frame and mailing protocol followed"), and by the calculations of Rita Simon and Valerie Hans, the social science research directors to the Special Committee on Gender.

Technical Appendices lists include people who may not have litigated in the D.C. Circuit courts during the last five years. 

In addition, neither list is likely to include all government attorneys who regularly litigate in the D.C. 

Circuit courts. 

We concluded that the best way to obtain a group of active litigators was to sample from those attorneys who had entered their appearance in at least one case in the District Court or the Court of Appeals during the year prior to the survey. Accordingly, the Systems Offices in both courts prepared lists of all such attorneys from docket sheet information, eliminating duplicate names and individuals appearing pro se. The lists contained a total of 4,289 men and 1,168 women attorneys. Questionnaires were mailed to every female attorney on the list and to a random sample of 1,268 male attorneys.

Two specialized pools of attorneys were identified as important court participants: the attorneys appointed to the Criminal Justice Act Panels in the District Court and the Court of Appeals, and the Assistant United States Attorneys. Questionnaires were sent to all members of these two groups who had not already been included through the general mailing. Some of the AUSAs are assigned to Superior Court rather than federal court. However, there is regular assignment rotation in the office, suggesting the value of including all AUSAS in the study. 

Thus, the questionnaire study constituted a random sample of male attorneys litigating in the D.C. Circuit courts, and a sample of all female attorneys, all CJA attorneys, and all AUSAs. 3.

Survey Procedure 

Each attorney received a questionnaire with a cover letter, signed by the judges who were members of the Task Force, explaining the purpose of the study and noting that all of the information would be treated as confidential. A return envelope with franked postage was also included. Return envelopes were coded with a four-digit number so that it would be possible to send follow-up letters only to those attorneys who had not yet returned their questionnaires. Two followup mailings were done. The first was a postcard urging respondents to send in their questionnaire and to call if a second copy was needed. In the second follow-up, attorneys who had not yet responded were sent another copy of the questionnaire, along with a new cover letter explaining the desirability of full participation. 4.

Response Rates 

A total of 2,749 questionnaires were mailed to attorneys, and 1,693 usable questionnaires were returned. The completed questionnaires included 718 from women attorneys and 

971 from men attorneys. Four respondents did not indicate their gender. To calculate the response rate, we subtracted from the total an additional 84 questionnaires that were returned but were C-2

Technical Appendices unusable. The overall response rate for the attorney questionnaire was 64%, considered to be a very good response rate for a mail survey. Response rates for various groups of respondents differed somewhat, but all groups surveyed in the study had acceptable response rates. Male attorneys responded at a 66% rate, compared to a 60% rate for female attorneys. The CJA attorneys' response rate was 73%, while the AUSA response rate was 58%. 

The sample of completed questionnaires may not precisely reflect the entire population of attorneys practicing in the D.C. Circuit courts. First, even though the response rate was very good, the views of those who chose not to participate in the survey could be significantly different from the attitudes of the participants. One possibility is that our participants felt more strongly about issues of gender in the courtroom and therefore had greater interest in completing the questionnaire. 

Second, because the attorneys in the general sample were identified from case docket sheets, those attorneys who work on federal cases but who do not enter an appearance are not represented. Third, we purposely oversampled women attorneys, CJA attorneys, and AUSAS. For instance, women attorneys contributed 43% of the completed questionnaires, but constituted only 21% of the names of the docket sheets. 

At the same time, the questionnaire results provide a significant body of valuable quantitative data. Because the response rates for both men and women were so good, they are of sufficient magnitude to permit inferences to be drawn about the characteristics and attitudes of the whole population of men, and the whole population of women, who are active litigators in the D.C. 

Circuit federal courts. In this Report, we discuss the views of the 1,693 attorneys who returned useable questionnaires. We also draw comparisons between characteristics and experiences of the men and women attorneys in the sample. In order to draw inferences about the overall characteristics of the entire population of active litigators, however, it would be necessary to weight the responses of the different groups in the sample by their proportion in the entire population.2 5.

Margin Comments 

Attorneys filling out the questionnaire were invited to write any specific comments about individual items, or other comments, on the questionnaire. Once all quantitative data had been entered and checked under the supervision of the Social Science Research Directors, the questionnaires were returned for compilation of the margin comments. Law students and paralegals 

So, for example, the responses of the women attorneys would have to be weighted so that their views are represented in proportion to their numbers in the overall population of active litigators (e.g., not 43% but rather 21%). Likewise, the responses of the CJA attorneys and the AUSAS would have to be weighted in proportion to their frequency in the overall population. Their proportions would have to be determined from the number of attorneys in each of these two groups that were included in the general sample. The preparation and analysis of weighted data could not be completed in the time available.

C-3

Technical Appendices recorded margin comments for each questionnaire, and when entering the comment, identified each with a code number and provided only the gender, and race/ethnicity of the respondent; if a margin comment related to a particular Survey Question, this was noted as well. Approximately 38% of the questionnaires contained margin comments. The margin comments were reviewed by Committee members responsible for drafting particular sections, and, after issuance of the Preliminary Report, were also reviewed and analyzed for the Committee by Professor Anita Allen, of Georgetown University Law Center. As noted in the Report, margin comments can be useful in illustrating or qualifying attorneys responses to questions but, as with other qualitative data, cannot be used to indicate the frequency of particular experiences. B. 

Employee Questionnaire

Beginning in the Summer and continuing through the Fall of 1993, the Committee, with assistance from its Social Science advisors and in conjunction with the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity, developed a questionnaire directed to employment experiences and observations. 

Design of the questionnaire was based in part on information about employee's perceptions and experiences identified in employee focus groups and interviews (described below); the questionnaire also sought to explore employees' perceptions of some areas explored in the Attorney Survey. 3/ A single questionnaire, from both Special Committees, addressing issues of gender, race and ethnicity, was distributed to 390 courthouse employees beginning on February 14, 1994.3 

Employees were reminded by e-mail and other court communications of the importance of responding. In order to assure the confidentiality and anonymity of responses, questionnaires were received in the Task Force offices and shipped, unopened, to a data entry service which prepared the data runs from which all analysis was done. 

The Committee received 243 useable questionnaires, for a response rate of 62%. 

Court employees responded to the questionnaire in close proportion to their representation in the workplace. Over 50% of employees in each office responded. Sixty-two percent of all women employees responded, as did 63% of all male employees. Over one-third of the questionnaires contained marginal comments, which were collated and summarized by a student intern. 

3/ The 390 courthouse employees were all full and part-time employees, excluding Judges and Magistrate or Bankruptcy 

Judges. Neither the Office of the Federal Public Defender, nor the U.S. Marshal's Office or Office of Pretrial Services 

(though housed in the courthouse), were included in the Employee Survey.

Technical Appendices C. Demographic Data 

Existing demographic data were collected from a variety of sources. But much demographic information about the participation of women and men in various roles in the work of the courts had to be developed by the Committee through identification of the gender of those who serve in various capacities. Specific sources for figures discussed in the report are provided in the text and footnotes of the report, as are several tables reflecting data collection. This section provides background on some of the principal data collection projects of this Committee. 1. 

Court Advisory Committees: Committee members undertook to count the number of women and men and, where identification was possible, the number of women and men who were members of racial or ethnic minorities, who served on those committees of the courts of this Circuit whose membership was published in the Annual Reports of the Circuit from 1986-1992. 

Committee members also identified the gender of those who served as committee chairs, and, through research in Martindale-Hubbell, the year of law school graduation for many, though not all, committee members. 2. 

Judicial Conference Participation: Committee members and advisors, based both on lists of invitees provided by the Circuit Executive's office and on the published Judicial Conference pamphlets for 1985-1992, were able to identify the gender of those (1) invited to the Judicial Conference, (2) who served on the Committees of the Conference, and (3) who served as panelists at public panels of the Conference. 

3. Special Masters: Beginning in the summer of 1992, and continuing in early 

1993, two law students working under the supervision of the co-chairs and the Executive Director identified all cases beginning in 1980 in which a "special master" was appointed. More detailed information about the nature of the search is provided in Technical Appendix II-A below. 4.

Independent Counsel: Researchers were unable to identify from available courthouse records information concerning the number of appointments made under the statute. 

Accordingly, researchers identified investigations and appointments from newspaper reports, and information as to the overall number and gender of those appointed was also provided by staff with a congressional oversight committee. 

5. Alternative Dispute Resolution panels: From information provided by the 

Alternative Dispute Resolutions Office, the Committee was able to identify the numbers of men and women trained for each of the Circuit's dispute resolution programs, and the number currently serving.

6.

Criminal Justice Act Panels: From lists of the courts' Criminal Justice Act panels and other information supplied by the Federal Public Defender, and from data supplied by offices of the two courts concerning appointments of CJA attorneys and payments of vouchers, the Executive Director of the Task Force coordinated compilation of demographic information about the attorneys appointed by the courts to serve as CJA panel attorneys; about the number of appointments of different types received by those attorneys; and about payment of their vouchers. The data are summarized in Chapter IV of the report.

Jurors: The Jury Office provided the Task Force with copies of Forms JS-12, identifying the demographics of samples drawn from current and immediate past jury wheels. A student intern, under the supervision of the Task Force Executive Director and the Jury Office, reviewed three months of juror questionnaires, compiling information on excuse patterns. For each potential juror, the intern recorded the nature of the excuse sought, whether it was granted or denied, as well as basic demographic information about the individual, e.g. gender, race, ethnicity. To preserve the confidentiality of potential jurors, they were identified by number only. The review was conducted in the courthouse. Additional information was obtained from research done by another law student, with assistance from the Jury Office, of jury selection in civil and criminal trials held over a six month period.

Pretrial Release: The Committee relied on two principal sources of information concerning pretrial release decisions in this district. Technical Appendices 13. Sentencing, Plea and Trial Rates: The U.S. Sentencing Commission generously responded to this Committee's requests for information on many occasions, with data sheets concerning defendants in this District and nationally which Committee members then analyzed. 

14. Employment Discrimination Case Review: The Equality Law Subcommittee organized a study of published opinions on motions to dismiss, summary judgment, and bench trials in all employment discrimination cases decided within the D.C. Circuit in the preceding five year period. Data was coded to permit analysis of different kinds of claims (e.g., age discrimination as compared with gender discrimination claims), at different stages (e.g., summary judgment as compared with trial), against different kinds of defendants (e.g., the federal government as compared with private employers or local governments), brought by plaintiffs of different gender and racial/ethnic identity (e.g., women compared with men, or minority women with minority men), and prosecuted by counsel or pro se. Analysis of the data that was collected could not be completed in the time allowed. For a more detailed description, see Technical Appendix III-A below. D. 

Qualitative Data: Interviews and Focus Groups 

1. 

Focus Groups: Focus groups are useful tools of research, generally consisting of 6-10 persons who participate in a discussion of particular general topics. Focus groups can be a rich source of data, particularly concerning interactive experiences. As noted in the Report, participants in a focus group are not limited to pre-set choices, as in responding to a written questionnaire. They may fully explain their responses to others in the group; moreover, hearing others describe their experiences often prompts participants to reflect on events in their own lives. 

Exchange between participants, and the opportunity to challenge and qualify statements made by other members of the group, can create a more detailed and clear picture of the phenomenon under study. Focus groups also have limitations. Participants may be hesitant to discuss sensitive topics, or they may not be as frank as they would be on an anonymous questionnaire. Most important, because focus groups consist of small numbers of participants who (as here) are asked to or (in other studies) volunteer to take part, and because they report qualitative information about their own personal experiences, they do not allow a researcher to reach conclusions about the overall incidence of such experiences among lawyers in the circuit. Focus group data is ideally combined with other research approaches, such as questionnaires completed by random samples of lawyers, as the Committee did in preparing the Report. 

As noted in the Report, focus group members were selected by Committee members, by the Executive Director and by other attorneys prominent in their fields of practice, and efforts were made to include attorneys whose background and practice might provide different kinds of perspectives. 

Committee members and the Executive Director reached out beyond those whom they knew to identify experienced attorneys in particular substantive fields of practice (such as communications, energy, environmental, bankruptcy or employment discrimination law) who were willing to volunteer C-8

Technical Appendices their time, both to participate in focus groups themselves and to identify and recruit other experienced practitioners to participate. "Purposive selection" of participants based on their particular areas of expertise, or demographic characteristics, is well accepted, as is the "snowballing" technique of calling on those in a particular area to identify others. 

Between February 1993 and April 1994, 19 focus groups of attorneys were held by this Committee, most of them devoted to issues of gender and some of them devoted to issues of gender and race and ethnicity. The Special Committee on Gender had the benefit of 6 general litigation focus groups (including one focus group that consisted only of male attorneys, and another that consisted only of minority women attorneys), 3 focus groups in areas of administrative practice (environmental law, energy law, and communications law), 2 focus groups of criminal defense attorneys, one focus group of AUSAS, two focus groups of bankruptcy attorneys (one consumer, the other business), and 5 focus groups of employment law practitioners. Some of these groups were tape-recorded, but in all instances a note-taker, usually an attorney or law student, was present to take notes. In addition, this Committee had the benefit of some attorney focus groups held by the Race and Ethnicity Committee.

In addition, employees of the courts were offered the opportunity to participate in focus groups. Approximately 65 employees participated in 10 different focus groups held between 

March and August 1993 that were facilitated by Committee members, and another 3 focus groups of employees were facilitated by members of the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity. Some focus groups were held at the courthouse, and some were held away from the courthouse. Some of the groups were tape-recorded, but in all an attorney was present solely to serve as note-taker. 

Participation was encouraged by the Chief Judges of the District Court and Court of Appeals. See 

Appendix F. 

2. 

Interviews: Committee members conducted interviews with a wide variety of participants in the work and worklife of these courts. As described in the text, Committee members individually interviewed all 16 courthouse managers, and conducted a round-table discussion with five supervisors.

Judges: While some other Task Forces had administered written questionnaires to judges, the small size of this Circuit made it possible for the Committee, working with the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity, to conduct individual interviews with all judges of the courts

Technical Appendices interested in participating. The two special committees worked together to develop questions for those interviews and a procedure that would meet needs for efficient use of time and confidentiality of data. Judges were provided in advance of the interviews a written list of questions (see Appendix 

G), and participation was entirely voluntary.

In order to assure that separate focus was brought to issues of gender and to issues of race and ethnicity, each interview was conducted by a representative from the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity and a representative from this Committee. The interviews were scheduled for a one-hour block of time, divided equally between the two interviewers, and the two Special Committees alternated which interviewer would go first. Many judges spent more than the allotted time. Generally there was only one interviewer in the room at a time. 

The Executive Director of the Task Force served as note-taker, and prepared written summaries of each interview (in which judges were identified only by a code, not by name). These written summaries were then reviewed for accuracy first by the interviewers, and then by the judge, before being finalized. A summary of areas covered by the interviews was prepared by the Executive 

Director and circulated to Committee members. At the time of the interviews, the District Court consisted of ten active judges, seven senior judges and three magistrate judges; there was one judge on the Bankruptcy Court. Overall, 12 interviews were held with these trial level judges, of which two were with senior judges. During the same time, there were ten active judges on the Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit. Three individual interviews were held, and two additional judges submitted brief written responses addressing one or more of the questions. E.

Other Data Gathering: With cooperation and assistance from the Federal Bureau of 

Prisons, Committee members made field visits to three federal prisons (the women's prison at Alderson, and two men's prison facilities in Petersburg), as well as to two Community Corrections Centers in the District, one for men and one for women. Committee members observed the facilities, and conducted interviews of inmates at the facilities who were willing to be interviewed. The results of those interviews and field visits are reported in Chapter IV. 

F. Public Notice: On January 31, 1994, the Committee mailed to over 700 organizations and potentially interested individuals a brief notice, describing the work of the Special Committee on Gender and soliciting input into areas warranting research. A similar notice was published on 

February 14, 1994 in the Daily Washington Law Reporter. See Appendix H. In response to these notices, the Committee received approximately two dozen written comments, most directed to the importance of the Committee's research or the scope or emphasis of its inquiry (e.g., desirability of gathering data on hiring or appointments by the courts, or of exploring such issues as sexual C-10

Technical Appendices orientation bias, gender bias in administrative proceedings, and the combined effects of race and gender).

TECHNICAL APPENDIX II-A: (Special Master Study) 

We initially sought to examine cases active from 1980 to February 1993. "Active" cases are those involving reported or readily identified litigation activity within that time period. We used research into legal sources and data bases to identify cases referring to "special masters" within this Circuit, and examined the cases to further identify those in which special masters had actually been appointed. In addition, at the Executive Director's request, the District Court Clerk's office made informal inquiries of its own employees and the judges' staffs to identify other cases in which special masters were appointed. All docket sheets for these cases were pulled and reviewed to determine the gender of the special master(s). There were several cases for which no docket sheet could be located, and these cases were not counted in the overall sample. A total of 32 cases active between 1980-January 1993, with 57 appointments, were identified in this manner. 

A report prepared in 1992 for Stephen Saltzburg, Reporter of the Civil Justice Reform Act Advisory Group, then came to the attention of the Special Committee. This letter identified 18 cases, which it described as "recent cases involving special masters or referees in this district." Five of these cases bore reported decision dates in 1978-79; the remainder bore reported decision dates from 1980 forward, and had all been identified by our earlier research. Because of the CJRA group's consideration of the five 1978-79 cases, we included the additional five cases in the total examined 37 cases and 62 confirmed special master appointments. We have identified the date of appointment of these cases, and in some of the cases "active" in the 1980s the appointment of the special master was made prior to the appointment dates of the five additional cases identified in the report to the 

CJRA group.

APPENDIX II-B: Mandatory Invitees To The Judicial Conference 

Mandatory Invitees:

By statute, all actively serving circuit, district and bankruptcy judges must attend the Judicial Conference. 28 U.S.C. § 333 (1988 & Supp. V 1993). By local rule, a lengthy list of other mandatory invitees is provided. See D.C. Cir. R. 47.3 (Dist. of Columbia Court Rules

Annotated (1994 Vol. 2) (requiring that invitations issue to senior and retired judges, magistrate judges, the Circuit Executive and the clerks of the Circuit, District and Bankruptcy courts, the Chief 

Staff Counsel and Chief U.S. Probation Officer, the Chief Judge of the Tax Court or a representative, the Director of the Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts, the Director of the Federal Judicial 

C-11

Technical Appendices Center, the U.S. Attorney for D.C., the Federal Public Defender for D.C., the Director of the D.C. 

Public Defender Service, the Director of the Administrative Conference of the United States, the deans of the law schools in the District of Columbia, the Corporation Counsel for D.C., and an unspecified number of members of the bar, and other individuals "whose background, position or achievement will contribute to the purpose and program of the conference"). Chief Judges of the other Courts in the District of Columbia, the Attorney General of the United

General in charge of the Civil, Antitrust, Civil Rights, Criminal, and Lands and Natural Resources Divisions. Until recently, the Local Rule had also required that the number of bar members invited be not less than the number of mandatory invitees, and that at least one-fourth of the invited bar members were to be in U.S. government service. Technical Appendices TECHNICAL APPENDIX III-A: Employment Discrimination Case Study Methodology 

The Case Study examined employment discrimination cases decided within the D.C. 

Circuit in the five years before the Study was launched. It was designed to enable the Committee to compare success rates for plaintiffs and defendants in different ways. The Committee could compare how plaintiffs had fared on different kinds of discrimination claims (e.g., gender-based, age-based, race-based). It could also examine success rates at separate stages of litigation (e.g., motions to dismiss, decisions after bench trials), and with respect to actions against different kinds of employers (private, local government, federal government). The data also enabled the Committee to compare the success rates of plaintiffs of different genders and/or races/ethnicities. 8/ The data pool consisted of published" opinions, issued from 1988 through mid-1993, by the D.C. District Court in employment discrimination cases. Only opinions deciding either a dispositive motion or a case after a bench trial were included in the pool. A second data pool consisted of published opinions issued in the same time period by the D.C. Circuit in appeals from decisions on dispositive motions or bench trials.2 9/ We defined "Published" opinions as those which appear in West's printed reporters, F. Supp. and F.2d;

two specialized employment reporting services, Federal Employment Practice Cases (BNA) ("FEP Cases") and Employment 

Practice Decisions (CCH) ("EPD"); and the Lexis and Westlaw computer data bases. ม We decided to review all published opinions deciding dispositive motions (i.e., motions to dismiss, motions for summary judgment) in addition to opinions following bench trials because more than 95 percent of civil cases are resolved prior to trial in federal court. Accordingly, a data base limited to trial decisions would have resulted in a limited picture of actual outcomes in employment discrimination cases. On the other hand, we decided to limit our review to motions that could result in the final disposition of a claim, and to exclude from the study all decisions on other, non-dispositive motions (e.g., motions to compel discovery, motions in limine regarding admissibility of evidence). This decision would allow us to compare the relative bottom line success rates of various plaintiff groups. Every dispositive motion represents a hurdle in a case where a claim could be disposed of entirely in favor of one party, or could survive to go on to the next stage of litigation. Decisions on such motions would enable us to calculate percentages of successful outcomes for various plaintiff groups. For example, if defendant files a motion to dismiss and that motion is denied, this is a successful outcome for plaintiff and a loss for defendant, because the claim survives and plaintiff may proceed to trial. Conversely, if defendant's motion is granted, it constitutes a successful outcome for defendant and a loss for plaintiff, who has no further recourse (unless he or she appeals).

In contrast, it would be virtually impossible to assess the impact of decisions on non-dispositive motions on the ultimate success or failure of a claim. We therefore excluded decisions on non-dispositive motions from our data base. لو The Committee collected data and had it computerized for both data pools. It was able to perform a preliminary analysis of the data from the District Court data pool, but was not able to do so for the D.C. Circuit data pool. The description of the Study methodology, infra in text, therefore discusses only the District Court methodology, but the forms used for both pools are attached. C-13

Technical Appendices 

For purposes of the Study, the Committee used two different definitions of the unit for which outcomes would be measured. First, the "per claim" method, in which we separately analyzed the outcome of each individual claim considered in an opinion. A claim was defined as a challenge by one plaintiff against one defendant concerning one defendant-employer action (e.g. a failure to hire), which the plaintiff claimed to be based on one type of discrimination (e.g., genderbased), and challenged under one statute (e.g., Title VII). Under this definition, each claim could theoretically constitute a single lawsuit entitling plaintiff to relief. Second, the "per plaintiff" definition, in which we analyzed whether plaintiff prevailed on at least one claim -- either of all claims considered by the judge in that opinion, or of all claims of a given kind (e.g., gender-based claims) ruled on in that opinion.

We developed a data collection form that requests information for each claim regarding all facts needed to make the comparisons, including: the case citation; the gender and race or ethnicity of the plaintiff, whether the plaintiff was pro se or represented by counsel; the status of the defendant as a private or D.C. or federal government employer; the specific discriminatory act alleged (e.g., hiring, promotion, termination); the discriminatory basis alleged (e.g., gender, race, national origin, age, retaliation); the statute under which the particular claim is brought (e.g., Title 

VII, Age Discrimination in Employment Act, D.C. Human Rights Act); the posture of the case (e.g., decision on a motion to dismiss, decision after trial); and the outcome of the decision as to that claim (e.g., in favor of plaintiff or in favor of defendant). See attached District Court Case Information Form.

One form was completed for each claim in the Study. The information collected was then entered into a data base program created on a pro bono basis by computer specialists.10/ 

The computer data base permits a wide variety of comparisons, including the following: (1) success rates in cases brought by different groups of plaintiffs with respect to particular bases for discrimination claims (e.g., comparing the success rates of women and men on age claims, race claims, or gender claims, either on a per plaintiff or per claim basis); (2) success rates of different groups on a per plaintiff basis (e.g., examining the success rates of women and men by comparing the percent of each group prevailing on at least one claim of all claims at issue in the opinions under review); (3) success rates for plaintiffs and defendants in suits against the federal government as compared to suits against a private employer or local government; (4) success rates on different kinds of claims (e.g., race discrimination or gender discrimination), at separate stages of the litigation (e.g., summary judgment or bench trial), and for plaintiffs of different gender, race and ethnic groups (e.g. men and women, or minority men and minority women); and (5) success rates where plaintiff was represented by counsel and those where plaintiff appeared pro se. As noted in 10/ This time-consuming and difficult task was directed by Mr. Craig Dye, with the assistance of Ms.

Cindy Rodda C-14

Technical Appendices APPENDIX III-B 

Using LEXIS, a list of all 304 published decisions in the D.C. Circuit in 1993 was generated. 

Published decisions were chosen in order to (a) have a manageable sample and (b) because it seemed likely that the published opinions were, on the whole, of greater importance and visibility than the unpublished opinions.

Cases not involving administrative review or FOIA issues (e.g., 71 criminal cases, 29 disputes between private parties (e.g.diversity, ERISA), employment law cases, claims against the D.C. government, tax cases) were eliminated. The ensuing list of 162 administrative law case was crosschecked by a WESTLAW search of "administrative law and procedure," which added one case. 

The list of 162 was further pared down to 145 by eliminating 17 cases which did not appear to have been argued orally.

The courtroom minutes maintained by the Clerk's Office of the Court of Appeals were examined to determine the identity and gender of the oral advocates; ambiguities were resolved by doublechecking in the LEXIS and F.2d/F.3d reports. Seven of the cases were checked only in the published reports. 

Of the 145 administrative law cases argued in the D.C. Circuit and decided with published opinions in 1993, 91 were on direct review from an agency, and 54 were appeals from the district court. 

A total of 56 women attorneys (15.9%) and 297 men attorneys (84.1%) were oral advocates in these cases. Of the government attorneys, 42 were female and 119 were male. Of the non-government attorneys, 14 were female and 178 were male. 

APPENDIX IV-A: 

A law student paper analyzed jury selection in all criminal and civil jury trials in the District of 

Columbia between January and June, 1993. K. Cipriani, "Three Strikes: The Impact of Gender on 

Jury Selection," (Feb. 1994) (forthcoming in American Criminal Law Review) (on file with Task

Force office). Based on preliminary data presented therein, and at the request of the Committee, the student researcher sought to identify from accessible court records the gender of the attorneys for the parties in all the criminal jury trial cases. Using the Jury Office's sheets and the Clerk's Office's computerized docket, the student noted down the name of each defense attorney and prosecutor for whom a name was given in each of the 74 criminal juries selected during this six month period for which such information was available. (In some cases, the Court's docket did not list the name of a defense counsel or a prosecuting attorney, but all information on attorneys' names available through the computerized docket was collected.) A total of 240 attorney appearances were noted, of which C-21

Technical Appendices 

70 were by women and 164 by men; with respect to six appearances the gender of the attorney could not be determined from the name. If an attorney appeared more than once, each separate appearance was counted separately.

APPENDIX IV-B: 

A law student paper analyzed a limited sample of files for criminal defendants in 1992. T.

Moriarty, Gender and Its Effect on Federal Pretrial Release Outcomes (Feb. 1994) (unpublished paper on file with Task Force office). The primary source of data was the Pretrial Services Agency (PSA) report found in the publicly available case jacket. Three qualifications about this data, apart from the small number of cases analyzed, should be noted. First, there were no data regarding a defendant's racial or ethnic background. To the extent that race and gender may interact in a particular way in pretrial release decisions, this data is insufficient to explore any such interaction. 

Second, an effort was made by the student to equalize the number of male and female defendants. 

Data were collected for all defendants for the first 100 cases filed in 1992. This represented all cases filed in the first two and a half to three months of 1992. Thereafter, data were collected for an additional period of roughly six months for cases with female defendants or co-defendants, in order to increase the number of female cases that could be compared with the male cases. 11/ The Pretrial Services Agency interviews most defendants prior to arraignment and provides information and recommendations to the courts to assist them in making their release decisions. SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX D.1 GENDER COMMITTEE ATTORNEY SURVEY WITH COVER LETTER

Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER 

Attorney Survey 

D.C. Circuit Task Force on Gender, Race and Ethnic Bias United States Courthouse Washington, DC 20001

June Dear Counselor: 

The Judicial Council of the District of Columbia Circuit established the Task Force on Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias, to determine whether and to what extent gender, race, and ethnicity affect the operations and proceedings of the federal courts of the District of Columbia Circuit. The D.C. Circuit is one of the first federal circuits in the nation to examine these important issues. The Task Force has appointed leading members of the bar and academics to the Special Committee on Gender and the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity to carry out its mandate. The findings and any recommendations of the Task Force will be reported to the Circuit Judicial Council for action. The work of the Task Force is expected to be discussed at the 1994 D.C. Circuit Judicial Conference. 

The Special Committee on Gender has prepared the enclosed questionnaire of lawyers' opinions and experiences, which focuses on gender issues. This survey is a significant part of the Task Force's efforts to study these issues. We are asking a representative sample of lawyers in practice before the U.S. Court of 

Appeals for the D.C. Circuit and the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia to participate in the survey. Your name was selected at random from court dockets. 

Please take a few minutes to complete the enclosed questionnaire and return it in the envelope provided at your earliest convenience. Based on our experience and on that of lawyers who have reviewed the questionnaire, we think you will find that filling it out is simple, interesting, and not too time-consuming. To ensure that the study accurately reflects the views of a cross-section of lawyers of the Circuit, it is important that we receive a large response. 

All of the data from the survey will be treated as confidential, and no information that might identify individual respondents will be reported. The results will be tabulated and reported in the form of statistical frequencies and averages. Your name will not be placed on the questionnaire. The return envelope has an identification number for mailing purposes only, and to ensure that you will not receive follow-up requests. 

We do recognize how valuable your time is and how valuable your response will be, and are most appreciative for your assistance in this important undertaking. If you have any questions, please write 

Katherine L. Garrett, Executive Director of the Task Force, at the address above, or call her at: 202/273-0477. лици нит Сим วน
