SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX F GUIDELINES FOR FOCUS GROUPS, EMPLOYEE INTERVIEWS

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER TASK FORCE OF THE D.C. CIRCUIT ON GENDER, RACE, AND ETHNIC BIAS GUIDELINES FOR FOCUS GROUPS 

I.

Introduction 

"Focus groups" are essentially group discussions focused on a particular topic or set of issues. They are relatively informal settings for discussion of issues of concern to several of our subcommittees.

Our social science consultants assure us that no special training is needed in order to conduct the focus groups convened at this stage of our work. We understand that the discussion can be facilitated by a leader whose role is to draw out the participants' views on particular topics, using broadly crafted questions to move the discussion along. 

This memorandum sets forth guidelines for conducting these focus groups. 

II. Purposes of Focus Groups 

Focus groups can be used for varied purposes in social science research. Information from focus group discussions does not provide statistical evidence, but can be used to illustrate and provide a human dimension to other statistical evidence. 

The focus groups to be conducted by the Special Committee on Gender will serve several purposes. First, they will assist the Committee in further developing issues to explore. Second, they will elicit concrete occurrences that may illustrate the phenomena of gender bias as experienced by its victims. Third, the focus groups should serve to energize and educate our own Committee members and members of the legal community in D.C. about the work of the Committee and about gender bias. 

III. 

Confidentiality

It is imperative that information collected by the Special Committee on Gender be treated with the highest degree of sensitivity so that confidences are maintained and identifying information is not revealed. Focus group leaders should begin by highlighting the fact that information elicited during the discussion will be used only by the Committee in performing its work. Moreover, leaders should clearly state that care will be taken so that information will be used only in ways that do not identify either the speakers or others. 9/24/93

IV. Structure and Conduct of Focus Groups A. Size and Makeup of the Group 

The optimum size for this type of informal focus group is six to ten participants. The 

Committee will recruit as focus group members both men and women of a range of ages who practice in the federal courts in the District of Columbia. Some focus groups may select participants from subgroups or practice specialties. 

B. Location 

Focus groups should meet in a comfortable conference room setting. The Committee will ask several firms whose members serve on the Committee to donate their conference room space. A focus group participant may also have access to a conference room. Contact the co-chairs, subcommittee chairs, or Katia Garrett for assistance in finding a location. C.

Duration 

Leaders and participants should set aside one and one-half to two hours for the focus group, with the understanding that the discussion will run for approximately 90 minutes. All participants should make a commitment to arrive on time and to stay for the duration of the group. Late arrivals or early departures are both very disruptive to the group. 

D. Role of Facilitator 

The leader or "facilitator" should lead the group through a series of questions, but must have sufficient flexibility to steer the discussion in the direction that the participants go. The facilitator should not express his/her opinions, but should try to maintain an atmosphere in which differing views can be expressed. If the leader has a clear point of view, some people may be reluctant to speak up. 

The facilitator should try to keep the group on track by seeking concrete examples and asking follow up questions where appropriate. Discussions should not dwell on "how much" questions, but rather on "under what circumstances" questions. It is especially important that the facilitator not allow one or two members of the group to dominate the discussion. 

E. Beginning the Discussion 

At the outset, all participants should introduce themselves with a brief selfdescription. Where possible, the group should be provided with a list of participants in advance to ensure that attorneys who may have professional or personal contacts with other 9/24/93 2

participants that might restrain their candor are not scheduled to participate in the same focus group. Examples of these contacts may include opposing counsel or a partner and an associate from the same firm.

F. 

Recording the Discussion 

A record should be made of the discussion. It may be easiest to tape record the session as long as the participants agree. The facilitator should also arrange to have a designated note-taker/recorder who will take notes, turn the tape, and deal with any technical problems that arise during the session. Following the session, the facilitator, with the assistance of the note-taker, should prepare a report of the group for the Committee. V. 

Conclusion 

This memo attempts to set forth general guidelines to conduct informal focus groups. 

The attached sheets may be useful guides to general questions the facilitators can ask participants to address. However, facilitators should feel free to add other questions as appropriate. For additional information regarding the conduct of focus groups, please contact the Committee co-chairs, subcommittee chairs, or Katia Garrett (202/273-0477) 9/24/93 3

UNITED STATES COURT OF APPEALS ABNER J. MIKVA CHIEF JUDGE MEMORANDUM DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT UNITED STATES COURTHOUSE CONSTITUTION AVE & THIRD ST. N.W. 

WASHINGTON, DC 20001-200 April 6

TO: Judges, United States Court of Appeals Judges, United States District Court United States Magistrate Judges 

Bankruptcy Judge Teel

The Circuit Judicial Council has appointed a Task Force to assess the impact of gender, race and ethnic background on both judicial proceedings and administrative operations in the District of Columbia Circuit. The study will include examining caserelated proceedings as well as employment practices. The work of the Task Force is being carried out by two special committees: 

Special Committee on Gender and the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity. 

the 

The Employee Worklife Subcommittee of the Special Committee on 

Gender is studying workplace issues within the Courthouse. In order to identify issues that are of common concern to employees, the Subcommittee is holding initial, confidential "focus groups, or "listening sessions," in small groups. 11 The attached memorandum announces the meeting dates of the focus groups and encourages staff participation. The memorandum also explains how to sign up for the sessions and whom to contact if there are any questions. 

As you may be aware, the United States Judicial Conference has recently passed two resolutions on this subject. One endorses encouraging each circuit to sponsor educational programs on how to be sensitive to concerns of bias based on race, ethnicity, gender, age and disability. The second endorses the merits of the circuit judicial councils conducting studies with respect to gender bias in their respective circuits.

The Task Force and the Circuit Judicial Council are committed to producing meaningful and responsive report and recommendations. 

We are fortunate to have an exceptionally talented and dedicated staff whose active and candid participation in these focus groups and in other aspects of the study is crucial to the success of the Task Force's work.

For these reasons, we request that you encourage members of your staff to participate in the focus group sessions. We believe that, in order for this project to be a success, in addition to endorsing the concept of the study, judicial officers must also encourage active participation in the study by members of our staff. 

Carrett Penn 

Chief Judge 

United States District Court

TASK FORCE OF THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT ON GENDER RACE AND ETHNIC BLAS United States Courthouse Washingtos, D.C. 20001 

MEMORANDUM 

To: 

Linda J. Finkelstein, Ron Garvin, Mark J. Langer, Nancy Lazar, Nancy 

Mayer-Whittington, Eugene Wesley, Jr.

From: Joyce Hens Green, United States District Judge, Task Force Chair Re: Employee Focus Groups and Listening Sessions Date: March 17, 1993 

As you know, the Judicial Council has appointed a Task Force to determine the impact of gender, race, and ethnic background on proceedings in and operations of the courts in the D.C. Circuit. The study is wide-ranging, looking at the litigation processes of the courts as well as the employment practices. The work of the Task Force is being carried out by two Special Committees: the Special Committee on Gender and the 

Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity, chaired and staffed by professors and practicing attorneys. Their work is coordinated and facilitated by Katia Garrett, the 

Executive Director, who works in the Courthouse. The remaining Special Committee chairs and members work outside the courts. 

The Employee Worklife Subcommittee of the Special Committee on Gender is studying workplace issues within the Courthouse. The Subcommittee plans to hold initial, confidential "focus groups" or "listening sessions" with small groups of court employees, to assist in identifying issues that are of concern to employees. As Chair of the Task Force, I urge you to encourage all interested employees to participate. 

The attached memorandum announces the focus groups and identifies several dates that are available for several initial groups of employees within the courts. It also explains how to sign up, and whom to contact if there are any questions. 

The D.C. Circuit is one of the first federal courts in the country to study these issues, and we are committed to producing a meaningful and responsive report and recommendations. We are fortunate to have an exceptionally talented and dedicated group of employees in the Court family, and their active and candid participation is important to the success of the Task Force's work. 

If you have any questions, please give me a call.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER APPENDIX G GUIDELINES AND QUESTIONS FOR JUDGES' INTERVIEWS

INTERVIEW PROTOCOL - JUDGES' INTERVIEWS 

Individual interviews are being sought with each of the judges in the D.C. Circuit federal courts to learn about their observations and experiences in court with issues of race, gender, and ethnicity. The experience of the CJRA Advisory Group last year in interviewing each of the District Court Judges was enormously beneficial to the Group's work. We are particularly eager for input from each of the Judges on their observations and to obtain their suggestions for the direction of the Task Force's work. The format for the interviews is described below: 1. 

Identity of interviewers:

Each interview has two parts: one on gender issues, one on race and ethnicity issues. 

The purpose of separating the issues is to facilitate deeper exploration of each. Each part will be conducted by a members of the respective Special Committee (Gender- Committee co-chairs Prof. Vicki Jackson and Prof. Susan Deller Ross, and 

Committee member Stephen Pollak, Esq.; Race & Ethnicity Committee co-chairs 

Prof. Todd D. Peterson, Vincent H. Cohen, Esq., Vanessa Ruiz, Esq., Joseph Sellers,

Esq.) Katia Garrett, the Task Force Executive Director, will be the notetaker at each interview. This format is modeled on that used by the CJRA Advisory Group in its interviews with the District Court judges. 2. 

Scheduling the interview. 3. 

Each judge will be contacted by Katia Garrett to determine the judge's interest and willingness to participate in the interview, and to schedule a convenient time for the interview with interested judges.

If an interview is arranged, a follow-up memo from Katia Garrett will confirm the date and time of the interview and the names of the interviewers. A list of interview topics/questions will be provided at this time. 

Length of interview.

In order not to impose too great a burden on the judge's schedule, a one-hour block of time for the interview will be requested, to be split equally between the Gender Committee and the Race & Ethnicity Committee, as described above. Of course, each judge would be able to set the length of the actual block of time for the interview. The interviewers would welcome additional time to permit fuller exploration of the issues. 1

4. 

List of questions: 

Lists of questions tailored to the different circumstances of appellate and trial-level judges will be prepared, drawn from the lists of questions that were circulated among the Task Force judges. While the lists of the Gender and Race & Ethnicity 

Committees contain overlapping questions, the interviewers are coordinating the questions asked at each interview to avoid duplication. As noted, the lists will be provided in advance to give each judge the opportunity to consider his or her responses. The questions are intended to be a framework for the interview, and the topics chosen are those we felt would benefit from the give and take of conversation The lists are not intended to constrain judges from raising other topics during the interview. 5. 

Written record of the interview:

To preserve confidentiality and confidence in the interview process, the following procedures will be observed: (a) Katia Garrett will serve as notetaker for all interviews. After each interview, she will prepare a written summary that does not identify the judge by name. For purposes of internal controls, a code will be assigned to each judge. The code list will be kept separately from the summaries. (b) Katia will provide the first draft of the written summary to the interviewer(s), who will review it for accuracy and completeness. After incorporating their changes, Katia will hand-carry a copy of the draft summary to the judge who was interviewed. The judge can then review the summary for accuracy and completeness, and Katia will make any changes needed. (c) A final copy of the written summary will be given to the judge and to the interviewers (listed in item 1). This summary will be provided only to the judge and the interviewers. (d) A report will be prepared based on all the interview summaries, reporting information in general terms, without attribution to or identification of a specific judge (e.g., "All judges but one indicated they are aware of the EEO procedures that are in place for courthouse employees.") This report will be circulated within the Special Committees, to be considered and used with other fact-finding information and materials in preparing subcommittee and 

Committee reports. 2

As a judge, you observe many different courtroom interactions involving both male and female parties. Have you observed differences in the ways lawyers or jurors respond to male and female parties --for example, as to their credibility, or as to the seriousness of claimed injuries? 

Are there any other areas in which you have observed possible differences in the treatment of men and women in the courtroom that you want us to know about? 2. 

Do you believe your own gender has affected how you are treated as a judge? 3. *4. 

In focus groups here and in other research, some attorneys have raised specific concerns about their perception that women are sometimes treated differently than men in the courtroom. What, if anything, would you think it appropriate for a judge to do: a) If the judge saw a lawyer in the courtroom calling female witnesses by their first names, and male witnesses by their last names? b) If the judge saw a male lawyer using terms such as "young lady" to address female counsel, or calling her by her first name, when all other lawyers were called by their last name? (၁ If counsel, of either gender, made sexually suggestive remarks to the courtroom clerk, or law clerk?

Are you aware of any EEO concerns that minority employees have? any EEO concerns that non-minority employees have? 5. 0. 

Turning now to juries, our research indicates that, in juror excuses handled by the jury office, women are more likely to request excusal for child care-related reasons than are men. When potential jurors come before you, are there any reasons for being excused from jury service that women give more often than men? That men give more often than women? How do you respond to these different kinds of requests? 

Do you have a dress code for attorneys in your courtroom or for your employees? What is it?

Do you think it is inappropriate for female attorneys to wear a pants suit in court? 1

7. (A) The choice of a law clerk is a very personal decision, and we understand that there are a 8. 9. 10. 11. 12. 13. 14. myriad of factors that go into clerkship selection decisions. Understanding this, do you think it is desirable to have as a goal that, over time, you would have significant numbers of both male and female law clerks? Or do you believe that considering gender in this way should not be relevant to the selection process? In your clerkship selection process, what, if any, role, does an applicant's gender play? (B) Turning now to appointments of attorneys as special masters, experts, or members of court committees, and invitations to the Judicial Conference, do you think it is desirable as a goal over time to include significant number of female as well as male attorneys in those positions? 

Or do you believe that considering gender in this way should not be relevant to these appointment or selection processes? In your own recommendations, invitations, or appointment of attorneys to such positions, what, if any role does gender play? 

Do you think your own gender affects the perception of how you handle gender discrimination cases?

In response to a question on the survey we sent out, many male and female attorneys responded that they thought a defendant in a gender discrimination case would be better served by a female attorney. Based on your experience, do you believe that is an accurate perception?

One other area has emerged in our research, and that is the possibly unique experiences of minority women. Have you observed African-American, Hispanic, or Asian women being treated differently -- either better or worse in the judicial process than others? Do you have any sense of how prosecutors exercise their discretion in charging and/or plea bargaining with female as compared with male defendants? 

How often have you been confronted with a pregnant defendant whom you have had to sentence? Do you have any information about the level(s) of care available to pregnant offenders in various Bureau of Prisons facilities? If so, have you ever made recommendations to the BOP concerning the institution to which such pregnant offenders should be sent? Have you ever made recommendations to the BOP concerning placement of offenders for any other reason(s)?

Are there any physical aspects of the courthouse environment -- safety issues, comfort issues or otherwise -- that you believe pose different kinds of concerns for females than for males? 

Finally, what changes do you see over the time you have been on the bench (or in practice) with respect to issues of gender in the federal courts? What do you think the future holds? Do you have any suggestions for issues that should be explored by the Task Force in the area of gender? 2

gase note that these questions are designed for use in oral interviews. If you feel that a question is clear, please feel free to rephrase the question or to clarify it in your response. 

1. We recognize that, as an appellate judge, you do not have the opportunity to see parties and witnesses that trial judges have. Understanding this, have you ever sensed - whether through review of briefs, records, or in oral argument that the gender of either a party, or a witness, affected how that party or witness was perceived or treated by lawyers, judges or jurors at the trial level? If so, please describe how. (Please consider criminal, civil, and administrative cases) 

As a judge, you observe many different courtroom interactions involving both male and female attorneys. Have you observed any difference in the way men and women attorneys behave or are treated in the courtroom? If so, please describe. (a) For example, have you perceived any differences in advocacy behavior or style between male and female attorneys? If so, what are they? (b) Have you ever observed any differences in your own mode of questioning female counsel compared to male counsel? If so, what are they? (c) If there is a woman advocate on one side, have you observed opposing counsel acting differently in the courtroom than if all counsel are male? If so, please describe. 

Do you believe your own gender has affected how you are treated as a judge? If so, please explain.

Are there any physical aspects of the courthouse environment - safety issues, comfort issues or otherwise that you believe pose different kinds of concerns for females than for males? 

If so, what are they?

Finally, what changes do you see over the time you have been on the bench (or in practice) with respect to issues of gender in the federal courts? What do you think the future holds? Do you have any suggestions for issues that should be explored by the Task Force in the area of gender? 2

Attached Request for Public Comment Date: January 31, 1994 The District of Columbia Circuit Task Force on Gender, Race and Ethnic Bias was established by the D.C. Circuit Judicial 

Council to examine whether and how gender, and how gender, race and ethnicity affect the work and worklife of the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals and of the United States District Court for the District of Columbia. In creating this Task Force, the D.C. Circuit has taken a leadership role among federal courts in seeking to assure the fair and equal treatment of all individuals who come before its courts, whether as employees, litigants, lawyers, jurors, witnesses or otherwise.

The Special Committee on Gender was established by the Task 

Force to identify areas appropriate for research and to carry out that research. We are dedicated to conducting a thorough and impartial exploration of the issues. By the attached notice, we seek your input, input we need to broaden and sharpen our understanding of whether and how gender affects the work and worklife of these courts and their many participants.

NOTICE FOR PUBLIC COMMENT SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON GENDER D.C. CIRCUIT TASK FORCE ON GENDER, RACE, AND ETHNIC BIAS The Special Committee on Gender of the  

Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias is seeking input from interested members of the public. Comments will be accepted in writing (by mail or fax) until 

February 28, 1994.

The Special Committee on Gender is examining the ways in which gender affects the work and worklife of the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals and the 

U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia. Subcommittees have examined demographics; litigation process; criminal justice; administrative law; federal equality law, class, race, and gender: and the court employee worklife. The work of the Special Committee has included a survey of litigators in the D.C. Circuit federal courts conducted earlier this year; focus groups of practitioners and employees of the courts, and other research projects designed to collect relevant data. The preliminary report of the Special Committee on Gender will be presented to the D.C. 

Circuit Judicial Conference in June, 1994.

The Special Committee would like to invite interested bar associations, other organizations, and members of the public to submit written comments concerning the role of gender in the work and worklife of the D.C. 

Circuit federal courts. Comments should address the following issues: 

1. Does gender play a role in the decisions rendered by the D.C. 

Circuit federal courts? If so, how? 

2. Does gender play a role in determining the composition of the bar of these federal courts? If so, how? 

3. Does gender influence the treatment of participants in the criminal justice process in the D.C. Circuit federal courts? how? 

If 80. 

4. Does gender influence the treatment of participants in the civil litigation process in the D.C. Circuit federal courts? If so, how? 

5. Does gender play a role in administrative practice or proceedings that culminate with review in the D.C. Circuit federal courts? how? 

If so, 6. Are there other factors, such as race, ethnicity and class, which affect whether or how gender influences the work or worklife of the 

D.C. Circuit federal courts? 

7. What, if any, steps should the D.C. Circuit federal courts take to address any concerns relating to the study of gender bias that you may have identified?

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX B TECHNICAL APPENDIX

Appendix B Data Collection Methods 

The Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity used a range of data collection methods in order to study the issues within our mandate. The Committee worked with the Special Committee on Gender in a number of areas. Hence, some of the research described below was conducted by both Committees working together, some by the Special Committee on Gender, and some by the 

Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity. The procedures used are summarized herein. 

I. 

Demographic Data Collection 

Much

Limited existing demographic data were collected from a range of sources. demographic information had to be developed. Specific sources for figures discussed in the report are provided in the text and footnotes of the report. This section summarizes additional data collection projects. A.

Demographics of Court-Appointments We collected demographic information on the range of appointments made by the judges of the D.C. Circuit courts to court committees, alternative dispute resolution and Criminal Justice 

Act panels, and as Special Masters, and also studied invitations to the Circuit's Judicial Conference. 

1. Court advisory committees: Members of court advisory committees from 

1986 through 1992 were identified from the Circuit's annual reports. A total of 119 people had served on at least one committee during this time period. With the assistance of court staff, committee members, interns, and volunteers, the Committee identified the gender, race, and ethnicity of committee members and chairs, and sorted and collated the information. 2. 

Alternative dispute resolution panels: From lists supplied by the 

Alternative Dispute Resolution Office, and with substantial assistance from the Director, Deputy Director, and Secretary of the ADR Office, the Committee was able to identify the gender, race, and ethnicity of all but one of the 236 people who were serving on the courts' four ADR panels as of August 1994. 3. 

Special Masters: Melvina Ford and Jodie Foster, research assistants who worked with the Special Committee on Gender in 1992 and in early 1993, collected information about appointments of special masters in the district court from docket sheets, published decisions, and other information compiled by the district court Clerk's Office. The Special Committee on 

Race and Ethnicity sought assistance from members of both Committees' members, and leaders of the Bar for assistance identifying the race and ethnicity of the special masters who had been appointed. There were a total of 52 special master appointments made in 32 cases between 1980 and January 1993. Seven of these appointments were of U.S. Magistrate Judges; 45 were of private attorneys. Of the private attorneys appointed, we identified the race and ethnicity of 33: 

28 white and 5 minority.

4. Judicial Conference invitees: From lists supplied by the Circuit 

Executive's Office, the Committee compiled a single master list of all people who had been invited to the Circuit's Judicial Conferences from 1988 through 1992. In an effort coordinated by the Executive Director of the Task Force, this list of 1,082 people was circulated to members of both Committees and to leaders of the Bar for assistance in identifying the race and ethnicity of the individuals with whom they were familiar. All but 70 were identified. 5. 

Criminal Justice Act panels: From lists of the courts' Criminal Justice Act panels supplied by the Federal Public Defender, the Executive Director coordinated compilation of demographic information of the attorneys appointed by the courts to serve as CJA panel attorneys. The race and ethnicity of all 105 attorneys on the district court panel was identified, and of all but four (two men and two women) of the 109 attorneys on the court of appeals' CJA panel. B.

Demographics of Parties and Jurors 

1. 

Jury Pool Information 

The Jury Office provided the Task Force with copies of Forms JS-12, identifying the demographics of samples drawn from the current and immediate past jury wheels. The Office also provided information on the number of grand jurors and jurors attending in 1993, and a range of additional information, as requested.

A student intern, Andrea Mertz, conducted a review of three months of juror questionnaires, compiling information on juror excuse patterns. Ms. Mertz recorded for each prospective juror information about the nature of excuse sought, whether it was granted or denied, as well as basic demographic information about the individual, including gender, race, ethnicity, age, etc. To preserve the privacy of the potential jurors, they were identified by number only. 

The review was conducted in the courthouse, under the supervision of the Task Force Executive 

Director and the Jury Office staff. 

A second student intern, Patricia Hafener, reviewed juror questionnaires that had been returned over the course of six months, to identify all those of Hispanic origin, and all those with Hispanic surnames. Ms. Hafener recorded information about juror qualification and excuse, in order to determine whether there were differences in rates of excuse that correlated with Hispanic origin. To preserve the privacy of jurors, they were identified by juror number only. The review was conducted in the courthouse, under the supervision of the Task Force Executive Director and the Jury Office staff. Additional information on the jury-eligible population of the District of 

Columbia was obtained from the U.S. Bureau of Census. -2-

2. 

Pro se Party Demographic Information 

No information is kept on the gender, race or ethnicity of parties filing in either the district court or the bankruptcy court. Systems Offices from both courts produced lists of all pro se parties who had filed in the respective courts in 1993, providing the name and address of the individual, if that was in the public records. Pro se petitions filed by prisoners were not included. 

In 1993, there were 186 pro se petitioners in bankruptcy court, and 355 pro se nonprisoner cases in district court. Of the 1993 district court pro se cases, 38% (73) were filed by people providing addresses in the District of Columbia. An additional 23% were filed by people with addresses in Maryland or Virginia, and 35% provided addresses from other states or countries. Demographic data were neither sought nor obtained for plaintiffs providing addresses outside of D.C. as the goal of this project was, in part, to identify patterns of court access within the D.C. communities. Two of the district court cases were filed by a couple residing at the same address, and six bankruptcy court petitions were filed jointly by spouses residing at the same address. Each of these was counted as a single case, and a single plaintiff/petitioner for the race and ethnicity analysis that is described below. a.

Collection of race and ethnicity information - docket review Sheila Driscoll, a volunteer law student, obtained 1990 census block and housing unit information for each pro se individual residing in the District of Columbia, to obtain an overall demographic "snapshot" of the community members who file pro se. Information was located for 

72 of the district court plaintiffs (including the two couples) and for 162 of the bankruptcy court petitioners (including five of the six couples filing jointly). For each block, the census data provided the number of persons and the percentage in each racial group, and of Hispanic origin. 

In consultation with statistician and labor economist Marc Bendick, it was determined that from this information we could describe the average race and Hispanic origin of the pro se parties who had actually filed in the respective courts in 1993. b.

Collection of gender information - docket review Each party was assigned a gender status, based on name recognition. The eight couples in both courts were designated "J" and those individuals whose names were not gender-specific were designated "U." Among the district court pro se plaintiffs, as noted, two cases were filed by couples (one man and woman each). Of the remaining 70 cases, 21 were filed by women (30% of the 70 single-plaintiff cases), 47 by men (67% of the single-plaintiff cases), and in two, the gender of the plaintiff could not be determined from the name. 

Among the 186 bankruptcy court pro se cases, race and ethnicity data were located for 162 cases. Gender information was calculated for the total pool of pro se petitions and for the smaller -3-

pool of cases for which race and ethnicity data was known. In the total pool of 186 bankruptcy court pro se petitions, six were filed jointly. Of the remaining 180 petitions, 84 (47%) were filed by women, and 85 (47%) were filed by men. Gender could not be determined from the petitioner's name in 11 cases (6% of the total). C. 

Data limitations

There are several points to keep in mind while reviewing these data, as they affect any conclusions that can be drawn from the data to the population of pro se filers at large. First, the race and ethnicity information is an estimate of the populations as a whole. It cannot be broken down into whole numbers, as it is drawn from the percentage representation of the various racial and Hispanic/non-Hispanic groups, by block unit. The estimate is an appropriate and supportable one for purposes of describing the group of individuals for whom the data were gathered. 

Second, the determination of gender relies on name recognition, a method that is generally accurate. At the same time, since the data are not based on self-identification, they may be subject to error.

Finally, in order to understand how the pro se data compare with the overall caseloads in the Courts, we would need to know the gender, race, and ethnicity of plaintiffs and petitions who are represented by counsel. That would permit us to assess whether more of the plaintiffs/petitioners in general are men or women, African American, Hispanic, white or Asian American. We could also assess whether any group is disproportionately present among the pro se plaintiffs/petitioners. There are simply no data kept on these facts and, while the subset of pro se individuals was a manageable portion of the caseload permitting review, we lacked the resources to conduct the same kind of review for all other 1993 plaintiffs and petitioners. 3. 

Demographic Information on Defendants in Criminal Cases 

In projects that were relied on by both Committees, data from the U.S. Sentencing 

Commission and the District of Columbia Pre-Trial Services Agency were obtained and analyzed. C.

Additional Demographic Information 

Some demographic information is available, although it is not always complete or accurate. We obtained annual EEO reports from each of the court units in the D.C. Circuit, along with judiciary EEO reports from the Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts. Demographic information on the federal judiciary was obtained from the Federal Judicial Center, the Executive Office of the U.S. Department of Justice, and the Alliance for Justice in Washington, D.C. 

Information on attorneys in the D.C. area and nationwide was obtained from Census reports, and from other demographic sources, including surveys conducted by Of Counsel, Legal Times, and information gathered by the National Association of Law Placement. - 4 -

II. Survey Research 

Surveying groups by written questionnaire is a useful means to measure perceptions and the reported frequency of experiences. Written questionnaires are also useful for collecting demographic data to provide an understanding of a population as a whole. Questionnaires are subject to biases because they rely on self-reports of experiences, and on the perceptions of respondents. The Committee used several written questionnaires to explore areas of interest in its work.

A. 

Attorney Survey

In order to reach as wide a group of D.C. Circuit litigators as possible, the Committee worked with a social scientist to design a questionnaire that would gather relevant demographic and practice information about a sample of the courts' lawyers, and would obtain information about attorneys' experiences in and perceptions of the D.C. Circuit federal courts. In March 

1994, the Special Committee issued a questionnaire titled "Race & Ethnicity in the Federal Courts: 

Attorney Survey." 

1. Selection of the Attorney Survey Sample 

The questionnaire was designed for attorneys who had litigated in the federal courts of the D.C. Circuit within the five years preceding issuance of the questionnaire. We had two goals in our sampling strategy: a. b.

Insure that we were able to reach attorneys with a presence in the local legal community as well as actual federal court experience, representative of the cross-section of practice areas and lawyers who come before the D.C. Circuit federal courts.

Insure that we were able to reach minority attorneys as well as whites, and to do so in numbers sufficient to permit comparison between the experiences of minorities and whites, and, to the extent possible, between different minority groups.

There was no one source to which we could turn to identify all attorneys practicing in the D.C. 

Circuit federal courts, and no source at all to identify the race and ethnicity of attorneys practicing in these courts. Drawing on the experience of the Special Committee on Gender, we requested the Systems Offices of the courts to prepare a list of all attorneys who had entered an appearance in at least one case within the past year, excluding duplicate names and individuals representing themselves pro se. This produced a list of 2,933 names from the district court, and 2,165 names - 5-

from the Court of Appeals." Because a number of the questions on the survey were directed to the interaction between the federal courts and the D.C. area legal community, we determined to include only attorneys with offices in D.C., Maryland, or Virginia. This reduced the district court docket list by 214 names and the Court of Appeals list by 606 names. The sample construction insured that we would reach a large population of attorneys with current federal court experience, and with a presence in the area's legal community. 

The docket list was supplemented with four specialized groups who were determined to practice regularly in the D.C. Circuit federal courts: Assistant United States Attorneys for the

District of Columbia, Assistant Federal Public Defenders for D.C., attorneys in the D.C. Office of Corporation Counsel, and all attorneys on the courts' Criminal Justice Act panels. 

In an effort to ensure that a sufficient number of minority attorneys would receive the questionnaire, we contacted the area minority bar associations. Working with officers of the 

Asian Pacific American Bar Association, the Hispanic Bar Association, the Washington Bar

Association, the Greater Washington Area Chapter, National Bar Association, and the D.C. 

Chapter of the National Conference of Black Lawyers, the Executive Director identified the attorneys appearing on the docket lists of the two courts who were also a member of at least one of these minority bar associations. This yielded a list of 137 names.

Names were then randomly drawn from the docket lists to reach a total of 2,496 attorneys, each of whom was sent a questionnaire. The questionnaire study thus included a random sample of attorneys litigating in the D.C. Circuit courts, and a sample of all CJA Panel attorneys, all 

Assistant U.S. Attorneys, all Assistant Federal Public Defenders, all Assistant Corporation

Counsel, and all attorney members of area minority bar associations who had entered an appearance in the district court or court of appeals within the last year. 

2. Attorney Survey Procedure 

Each attorney on the mailing list received a copy of the survey instrument, a return envelope (postage pre-paid) marked with a four-digit mailing code, and a cover letter, signed by the Chief Judges of the Court of Appeals and the District Court and by the judge serving as the Chair of the Task Force, which emphasized the importance of the questionnaire and encouraged attorneys to respond. 1/ From the original district court list, we deleted 239 names of attorneys with the District of Columbia offices of the United States Attorney, the Corporation Counsel, and the Federal Public Defender, as well as all attorneys on the Criminal Justice Act panels, as these individuals were already included in our sample. For the same reasons, from the original 

Court of Appeals list of 2,849, we deleted 684 attorneys who met these criteria, or who were already included on the district court list. - 6-

Questionnaires were returned to the Task Force office in the U.S. Courthouse, where staff recorded the four-digit code as well as other relevant mailing information (e.g., questionnaires returned as undeliverable, and total numbers of questionnaires returned in envelopes other than those containing the four-digit code). Questionnaires were delivered, unopened, to the data entry service, Opportunity Systems, Inc.

There were three follow-up mailings. The first was a postcard sent to all attorneys on the mailing list, encouraging response. The second follow-up included a second copy of the questionnaire, a return envelope (postage prepaid) marked with the four-digit mailing code, and a second cover letter from the judges reiterating the importance of each attorney's response in the project. The final follow-up was a memorandum from the Executive Director encouraging attorneys to respond and offering to send another copy of the survey instrument. The last two mailings were sent only to those attorneys who had not yet responded at the time of the mailing, as identified by the four-digit mailing code on the return envelopes. 

Once all data had been entered and checked, the questionnaires were returned in numbered batches to the Task Force offices for coordination of the margin comment compilation. 

Individuals recording the margin comments assigned each questionnaire a code number, and when entering the comment, identified each with a code number that gave the gender, race, ethnicity, and area of practice of the respondent. Just over 50% of the questionnaires contained margin comments. 

3. Response Rates to Attorney Survey 

We sent 2,496 questionnaires, and received 1331 usable questionnaires in response. In addition, we learned from 230 attorneys that they had had no relevant federal court experience within the past five years. A number of these were from the Office of the Corporation Counsel, where attorneys had overall less federal court experience than had been anticipated. This group of 230 attorneys is appropriately deducted from the initial sample for purposes of determining the response rate. An additional 42 were returned by the Post Office as "undeliverable." Subtracting all unusable questionnaires from the total, the response rate is 1331/2266, or 59%. This response rate is roughly comparable to the rate of 64% achieved on the attorney questionnaire distributed by the Special Committee on Gender (n=2,479), and exceeds the rate of 52% achieved by the Ninth Circuit's Gender Bias Task Force (n=7,000), and the rate of 35.2% achieved by the local 

District of Columbia Race and Ethnic Bias Task Force (n=900). 

One factor may have affected the response rate to the attorney questionnaire: we drew our sample from the same sources used by the Special Committee on Gender when it designed and mailed its questionnaire barely one year earlier. There was substantial overlap among the attorneys receiving the two questionnaires. Although the cover letters made clear that this was a separate study, nevertheless, there may have been some confusion. The Executive Director -7-

received a number of calls asking for clarification from attorneys who said they had "already responded" or had "responded last year."

The sample of completed questionnaires may not be an exact reflection of the entire population of D.C. area attorneys practicing in the D.C. Circuit federal courts. We have no way of determining the extent to which the views of non-responding attorneys differ from or mirror the responses of the survey participants. It is possible that the people who did not respond felt less strongly about issues of race or ethnicity, or felt their range of experience made them less qualified than others to address the issues. In addition, as noted, the sample did not capture those attorneys who work on D.C. Circuit federal court cases but who do not enter an appearance in the cases.

At the same time, the questionnaire results provide a significant body of valuable quantitative data. The Committee's report discusses the views of the 1331 attorneys who returned usable questionnaires. It also presents comparisons between minority and white attorneys, between African American and other attorneys, and discusses and contrasts responses from minority women, minority men, white women, and white men. All of these groups are represented in the sample and among the respondents. In addition, because the response rate was very good -- well over 50% it was of sufficient magnitude to permit limited inferences to be drawn about the characteristics and attitudes of the population of D.C. Circuit federal court practitioners. B. 

Employee Questionnaire

A single questionnaire from both Special Committees, addressing issues of gender, race, and ethnicity, was distributed to all courthouse employees on February 14, 1994. Two follow-up deliveries were made, and employees were reminded via e-mail and other court communications of the importance of responding. Responses were completely anonymous. Questionnaires were received in the Task Force Office and shipped, unopened, to a data entry service, which prepared the data runs from which all analysis was done. 

The Committees received 243 usable questionnaires, for a response rate of 62%. Court employees responded to the questionnaire in close proportion to their representation in the workplace. Over 50% of employees in each office responded to the questionnaire. Similarly, over 50% of all minority employees responded (53%), as did over 60% of all white employees (68%). Sixty-two percent of all women employees responded, as did 63% of all male employees. 

Over one-third of the questionnaires (88 or 36%) contained margin comments, which were collated and summarized by Patricia Hafener, a law student intern working with the Task Force. - 8-

III. Qualitative Data: Focus Groups and Interviews 

A. 

Focus Groups 

S Both to inform the Committee in its development of questionnaires, and to supplement the survey findings, a number of focus groups were convened. These included groups of attorneys, community members, federal probationers, court employees from all of the court offices, and probation officers discussing issues unique to their clientele. Focus groups bring together a group of people to explore issues of mutual interest, in an informal discussion that generally lasts between one to two hours. The focus group process yields qualitative information, providing an anecdotal dimension to the data collected. A general list of questions for each group was prepared by the facilitator, based on a protocol developed by the Committee. Discussion leaders were 

Committee members or volunteer attorneys, each of whom was provided in advance materials explaining how to conduct focus groups as well as the purpose of the focus group discussions. 

Most of the groups also had a volunteer notetaker, who either prepared a written record of the discussion or reviewed a transcript. Identifying information was deleted from notes and transcripts, and the final versions were provided to the Committee members exploring issues covered in the discussion.

The anecdotal information developed in focus groups adds an important dimension to the quantitative data developed. The group discussions provide an opportunity to explore in depth the experiences of individuals in the various circumstances explored by the Committee: the courthouse workplace, the courtroom, and the professional federal legal community. The groups are each small, however, and generally include no more than ten people, all of whom were selected based on their relevant experience in the federal court or the federal legal community, or, for the community focus groups, in the local community. Focus group data, because they are not drawn from a random sample of the population as a whole, cannot provide reliable information about the extent to which the experiences of focus group members are representative of the population as a whole. At the same time, the focus group discussions did reliably report the personal experiences of the participants. Additionally, the participants were selected for their expertise and experience in the relevant areas under discussion, and were thus well-placed to discuss these issues. The experiences were valuable in directing specific research undertaken by the Committee, and in supplementing the quantitative research conducted. 

More than 150 attorneys, community members, and probationers participated in the groups, as did more than 60 court employees. Attorney participants were drawn from the private and public sector, from criminal and civil law, and from the defense, prosecution, and plaintiffs' 2/ For more discussion of focus groups, see Studying the Role of Gender in the Federal 

Courts: A Research Guide (Preliminary Version, June 27, 1994), Federal Judicial Center, at 

39-48, and materials referenced therein. -9-

bars. All courthouse employees were invited to participate, and efforts were made in structuring the groups to ensure representation and input from each of the offices of the Circuit. B.

Interviews The Committees jointly interviewed all Court Managers about courthouse worklife issues, and offered interviews to all of the active judges of the courts. Over 80% of the active trial-level judges, along with three of the appellate judges were interviewed. In each, lists of questions prepared by the Committee were provided to the individual ahead of time. The interviews were conducted by Committee members, and another individual who served as notetaker. The notetakers in managers interviews provided drafts for review and correction by the interviewers. 

Members of the Committees' Employee Worklife Subcommittees prepared written summaries of all the managers' interviews for use in development of the reports. 

The Executive Director of the Task Force served as the notetaker in all the judges' interviews, and after each interview, prepared and circulated notes to the interviewers for review and correction. No judge was identified in the interview notes. The notes were then provided to each individual judge for their review and correction, and final versions were prepared by the Director. A summary of the judges' interviews, which does not identify the individual judges, was prepared by the Director, and used by Committee members in their development of the report.

Interviews permitted in-depth discussion of a range of issues of interest to the Committees, providing greater detail and clarity than is possible through written surveys. The informal nature of the interviews, however, meant that not all issues were covered in each interview, and not all issues were addressed in the same manner in each interview. 

IV. 

Public Hearing 

The Committee on Race and Ethnicity convened a public hearing in May 1993 to obtain input from the legal and lay community about the manner in which issues of race and ethnicity arose in the context of the federal courts' work and worklife, and the issues deemed important for exploration by the Committee. Letters requesting input were sent to more than 200 bar associations and other professional associations that interact with the courts, to organizations that either work with federal court litigants or that litigate in federal courts, to the litigation office and division heads in federal agencies, to public officials, the deans of area law schools, legal clinics, law student associations, and to a range of civic and community groups. In addition, all judges of the circuit and district were notified of the hearing. A media release was widely issued and notice of the hearing was printed in the Washington Post. A one-page Notice of Hearing was posted in the public offices of the Courthouse and copies were available to the public. The 

Executive Director met with the leaders of area voluntary bar associations to answer questions - 10 -

about the hearing and to solicit input at the hearing.

Numerous follow-up phone calls were made J to identify people interested in participating in the hearing. Interested parties were provided with registration forms and were assigned a specific time to testify. The notice advised that arrangements could be made for confidential testimony as well as for the submission of written comments. While offers were made to provide language interpretation, only one request -- for sign language interpretation -- was made. Accordingly, the Task Force arranged for sign language interpreters to be available at the hearing. 

Testimony was taken from 8:30 a.m. until 6:00 p.m., from 16 witnesses; additional written materials were received from two organizations and one individual. A reporter transcribed the testimony, and a copy of the transcript and written materials submitted were made available for public review and copying. A copy of these materials is on file with the Task Force Office. 

Information gathered at the public hearing is, again, not based on a random sample of any population. It is, however, yet another source of data that can supplement the survey, focus group, interview, and other qualitative and quantitative data gathered. A number of the witnesses who testified were uniquely placed to address issues covered during the hearing: for example, the 

Corporation Counsel for the District of Columbia and the Federal Public Defender for the District of Columbia each could speak with authority of the matters within the purview of their respective offices. Many additional witnesses, some with substantial experience in the federal courts or the local community, and some with personal experience in cases in the D.C. Circuit federal courts shared with the Task Force their experiences and assessments of the role of race and ethnicity in the D.C. Circuit. 

- 11 -

Technical Appendices Employment Discrimination Case Study Methodology 

The Case Study examined employment discrimination cases decided within the D.C. Circuit in the five years before the Study was launched. It was designed to enable the 

Committee to compare success rates for plaintiffs and defendants in different ways. The 

Committee could compare how plaintiffs had fared on different kinds of discrimination claims (e.g., gender-based, age-based, race-based). It could also examine success rates at separate stages of litigation (e.g., motions to dismiss, decisions after bench trials), and with respect to actions against different kinds of employers (private, local government, federal government). 

The data also enabled the Committee to compare the success rates of plaintiffs of different genders and/or races/ethnicities. 

The data pool consisted of published opinions, issued from 1988 through mid- 1993, by the D.C. District Court in employment discrimination cases. Only opinions deciding either a dispositive motion or a case after a bench trial were included in the pool. A second data pool consisted of published opinions issued in the same time period by the D.C. Circuit in appeals from decisions on dispositive motions or bench trials." We defined "Published" opinions as those which appear in West's printed reporters, F. Supp. and F.2d; two specialized employment reporting services, Federal Employment Practice Cases (BNA) ("FEP Cases") and

Employment Practice Decisions (CCH) ("EPD"); and the Lexis and Westlaw computer data bases. 11 We decided to review all published opinions deciding dispositive motions (i.e., motions to dismiss, motions for summary judgment) in addition to opinions following bench trials because more than 95 percent of civil cases are resolved prior to trial in federal court. Accordingly, a data base limited to trial decisions would have resulted in a limited picture of actual outcomes in employment discrimination cases. On the other hand, we decided to limit our review to motions that could result in the final disposition of a claim, and to exclude from the study all decisions on other, non-dispositive motions (e.g., motions to compel discovery, motions in limine regarding admissibility of evidence). This decision would allow us to compare the relative bottom line success rates of various plaintiff groups. 

Every dispositive motion represents a hurdle in a case where a claim could be disposed of entirely in favor of one party, or could survive to go on to the next stage of litigation. Decisions on such motions would enable us to calculate percentages of successful outcomes for various plaintiff groups. For example, if defendant files a motion to dismiss and that motion is denied, this is a successful outcome for plaintiff and a loss for defendant, because the claim survives and plaintiff may proceed to trial. Conversely, if defendant's motion is granted, it constitutes a successful outcome for defendant and a loss for plaintiff, who has no further recourse (unless he or she appeals). 

In contrast, it would be virtually impossible to assess the impact of decisions on non-dispositive motions on the ultimate success or failure of a claim. We therefore excluded decisions on non-dispositive motions from our data base. The Committee collected data and had it computerized for both data pools. It was able to perform a preliminary analysis of the data from the District Court data pool, but was not able to do so for the D.C. Circuit data (continued...)

Technical Appendices t in For purposes of the Study, the Committee used two different definitions of the unit for which outcomes would be measured. First, the "per claim" method, in which we separately analyzed the outcome of each individual claim considered in an opinion. A claim was defined as a challenge by one plaintiff against one defendant concerning one defendant-employer action (e.g. a failure to hire), which the plaintiff claimed to be based on one type of discrimination (e.g., gender-based), and challenged under one statute (e.g., Title VII). Under this definition, each claim could theoretically constitute a single lawsuit entitling plaintiff to relief. Second, the "per plaintiff" definition, in which we analyzed whether plaintiff prevailed on at least one claim either of all claims considered by the judge in that opinion, or of all claims of a given kind (e.g., gender-based claims) ruled on in that opinion. 

We developed a data collection form that requests information for each claim regarding all facts needed to make the comparisons, including: the case citation; the gender and race or ethnicity of the plaintiff; whether the plaintiff was pro se or represented by counsel; the status of the defendant as a private or D.C. or federal government employer; the specific discriminatory act alleged (e.g., hiring, promotion, termination); the discriminatory basis alleged (e.g., gender, race, national origin, age, retaliation); the statute under which the particular claim is brought (e.g., Title VII, Age Discrimination in Employment Act, D.C. Human Rights Act); 

the posture of the case (e.g., decision on a motion to dismiss, decision after trial); and the outcome of the decision as to that claim (e.g., in favor of plaintiff or in favor of defendant). 

See attached District Court Case Information Form. 

One form was completed for each claim in the Study. The information collected was then entered into a data base program created on a pro bono basis by computer specialists." 

The computer data base permits a wide variety of comparisons, including the following: (1) success rates in cases brought by different groups of plaintiffs with respect to particular bases for discrimination claims (e.g., comparing the success rates of women and men on age claims, race claims, or gender claims, either on a per plaintiff or per claim basis); (2) success rates of different groups on a per plaintiff basis (e.g., examining the success rates of women and men by comparing the percent of each group prevailing on at least one claim of all claims at issue in the opinions under review); (3) success rates for plaintiffs and defendants in suits against the federal government as compared to suits against a private employer or local (...continued) pool.

The description of the Study methodology, infra in text, therefore discusses only the District Court methodology, but the forms used for both pools are attached. 

This time-consuming and difficult task was directed by Mr. Craig Dye, with the assistance of Ms. Cindy Rodda

Technical Appendices government; (4) success rates on different kinds of claims (e.g., race discrimination or gender discrimination), at separate stages of the litigation (e.g., summary judgment or bench trial), and for plaintiffs of different gender, race and ethnic groups (e.g. men and women, or minority men and minority women); and (5) success rates where plaintiff was represented by counsel and those where plaintiff appeared pro se. As noted in the Report, the Committee was unable to complete its analysis of the data within the time allowed for completion of its work.

In 1988, six people were appointed to a single case, No. 88-625. These included one Hispanic male and one 

Hispanic female, one white male and three men whose race/ethnicity has not been determined. In 1989, an additional 

13 people were appointed as special masters in this same case. They included one Hispanic male, one Asian 

American woman, one white man, and nine men and one woman whose race/ethnicity has not been determined. b Reflects the appointments in case No. 88-625, in which 19 people were appointed as special masters. The first African American woman to serve as a U.S. magistrate judge in the District of Columbia from 1974 through 1992 began in 1988. U.S. magistrate judge from 1974-1992.

**NOTE FOR TABLES D-17 THROUGH D 20** 

The Gender Committee and the Race and Ethnicity Committee adopted different methods to compile the total number of judicial conference invitees. The Gender Committee counted a person each time he or she was invited to one of the conferences. The Race and Ethnicity Committee counted each invitee once, no matter to how many conferences he or she was invited.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX G THE ATTORNEY SURVEY* 

*The reminders regarding the Attorney Survey have been deleted to save space.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX G.1 RACE & ETHNICITY COMMITTEE ATTORNEY SURVEY WITH COVER LETTER

_202/273 FAX: 202/273-0659 

March 1994 

Dear Counselor: 

The Judicial Council of the District of Columbia Circuit established the Task Force on 

Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias, to determine whether and to what extent gender, race, and ethnicity affect the operations and proceedings of the federal courts of the District of Columbia Circuit. The 

D.C. Circuit is one of the first federal circuits in the nation to examine these important issues. The 

Task Force has appointed leading members of the bar and academics to the Special Committee on Gender and the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity to carry out its mandate. The preliminary report of the Special Committees will be discussed at the June 1994 D.C. Circuit Judicial Conference, and presented to the Task Force for appropriate action. 

The Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity has prepared the enclosed questionnaire of lawyers' opinions and experiences, which focuses on issues of race and ethnicity. This survey is a significant part of the Special Committee's efforts to study these issues. We are asking a representative sample of lawyers in practice before the U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit and the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia to participate in the survey. A similar questionnaire distributed by the Special Committee on Gender exploring gender issues received a significant response rate. Even if you responded to that questionnaire, your response is needed to this different questionnaire.

Please take a few minutes to complete the enclosed questionnaire and return it in the envelope provided at your earliest convenience. To ensure that the study accurately reflects the views of a cross-section of lawyers of the Circuit, it is important that a large response is received. 

All of the data from the survey will be treated as confidential, and no information that might identify individual respondents will be reported. The return envelope has an identification number for mailing purposes only, and to ensure that you will not receive follow-up requests. Neither your name nor the mailing identification number, nor any other information that could identify you, will be placed on the questionnaire. The results will be tabulated and reported in the form of statistical frequencies and averages.

We do recognize how valuable your time is and how valuable your response will be, and are most appreciative for your assistance in this important undertaking. If you have any questions, please write Katherine L. Garrett, Executive Director of the Task Force, at the address above, or call her at: 202/273-0477.

Севладинск 

Abner J. Mikva

Chief Judge, U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit 

John Garrett Penn

Chief Judge, U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia 

Joyce Hen. Green

Joyce Hens Green, Task Force Chair 

United States District Judge

Gender, Race, and Ethnic Blas

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY Race & Ethnicity in the Federal Courts: A Survey of Attorneys 

D.C. Circuit Task Force on Gender, Race, and Ethnic Bias United States Courthouse Washington, DC 20001

QUESTIONNAIRE FOR ATTORNEYS 

This questionnaire has been designed to minimize the time and effort required for you to complete it. Most of the following questions simply ask you to circle one or more responses, OR to provide a small amount of information. We recognize, however, that many of the issues addressed here are quite complicated. We also recognize that some of the items may not be appropriate for your particular position. Consequently, we encourage you to provide your comments, explanations, and other relevant information in the margins and on additional pages. 

All of the responses to the questionnaire will be treated as confidential, and no information that might identify individual respondents will be reported.

For purposes of this chart, the Court of Appeals' workforce includes employees in the Office of the Circuit Executive, Office of the Clerk of the Court, Circuit Library, Office of Chief Staff Counsel and chambers' staff (i.e., law clerks, judges' secretaries and the Administrative Assistant to the Chief Judge).

For purposes of this chart, the District Court workforce includes employees in the Office of the Clerk, Court Reporters and chambers' staff (i.c., law clerks, secretaries and the Administrative Assistant to the Chief Judge). It does not include employees in the Probation Office because that office, although a part of the District Court, is reported separately in this Chapter. 

For purposes of this chart, the Bankruptcy Court workforce includes employees in the Office of the Clerk and chambers' staff.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX K FOCUS GROUPS

8/13/

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY D.C. CIRCUIT TASK FORCE ON GENDER, RACE, AND ETHNICITY Guidelines for Focus Groups 

Several Subcommittees have expressed interest in gathering anecdotal information from different sectors of the legal community-- litigators, court reporters, employees, etc. 

A "Focus Group" is an effective means of gathering this kind of information, and has the added advantage of being relatively easy and inexpensive to organize and run. Here are some general guidelines that should help in this process. 

I. Introduction. "Focus groups" are essentially group discussions focused on a particular topic or set of issues. They are different from ordinary discussions because (1) participants have usually been chosen with research objectives rather than social or other considerations in mind; (2) the discussion is facilitated by a group leader who is there to promote interaction, draw people out, make sure that the discussion centers on the topic or set of issues, etc.; (3) the group leader has a prepared "protocol" or set of questions or issues used to guide the discussion; and (4) the discussion is documented for review and analysis. 

Focus groups are useful for three purposes. First, they will provide information about the experiences of participants in the Court system that will be used to help formulate questions for the surveys currently planned for attorneys and court employees. Second, they will provide anecdotal information which will give life to the "numbers" data being gathered. 

Third, the focus groups will energize and educate Committee members and members of the legal community about the work of the Task Force and about the role of race and ethnicity in the federal courts.

II. Confidentiality. It is imperative that information collected by the Special 

Committee on Race and Ethnicity be treated with the highest degree of sensitivity so that confidentiality is maintained and identifying information is not revealed. Focus group leaders should begin by highlighting the fact that information elicited during the discussions will be used only by the Committee in performing its work. Make sure to let participants know that names will not be used, and that information will be used only in ways that do not identify the speaker or others. Participants should also agree to honor this confidentiality -- the legal community here is fairly small. 

III. Structure and Conduct of Focus Groups. The groups are informal, and are not on the order of controlled social science studies. At the same time, there are some general guidelines that should be followed for each group. 1

8/13/1. Size and Makeup of the Group: In general, the group should include between six to ten participants. Smaller focus groups may be effective as well, but if a group is too large, you run the risk that some people may not participate. The key to a good focus group is establishing rapport and achieving a level of comfort that encourages candor. Because of the sensitivity of some of the issues being explored, you may want to consider structuring groups made up of participants of a particular race, gender, or ethnicity. Overall, we will strive to run focus groups with men and women representative of the demographics of the federal practitioners here; individual groups may be more homogeneous. 

2. Location: Groups should meet in a comfortable and convenient conference room. 

Committee members can hold the group at their own firm or can ask one of the participants to agree to host the group. Space can also be arranged at the Courthouse. Call Katia 

Garrett if you need help in finding a place for a group. 

3. Duration: Leaders and participants should set aside one and one-half to two bours for the group, with the understanding that the group will run for approximately 90 minutes. All participants should make a commitment to arrive on time and to stay for the duration of the group. Late arrivals or early departures are both very disruptive to the group. 

4. Role of the Facilitator: For the kind of informal focus groups planned by the

Committee, no social science expertise is needed. The group leader or "facilitator" should lead the group through a series of questions, but must have sufficient flexibility to allow the discussion to develop in the direction the participants go. The facilitator should not be "interviewing" the participants, but should be raising issues in ways that stimulate conversation. Several pointers: **use a written list of issues or questions (a "protocol") to guide the discussion. generally no longer than a page. This list should be developed by the Subcommittee as a whole to ensure clarity and consistency, and to ensure that the issues the Subcommittee is exploring are covered. You may want to distribute the list to participants ahead of time to permit them to think about some of the issues. **the facilitator should try not to express his or her own opinion, but should try to maintain an atmosphere in which differing views can be expressed. If a group leader has a clear point of view, some people may be reluctant to speak up. **keep the group on track by seeking concrete examples and asking follow up questions, where appropriate. Try to get participants to articulate the circumstances in which certain experiences occurred. 2

8/13/W 101 **don't allow one or two members of the group to dominate the discussion. Make sure every participant has a chance to speak up --- at the same time, try to let a discussion develop among the participants, avoiding an "interview" or "poll" approach. **try to stay away from buzz words, getting at conduct, comments, perceptions, etc. **hand out a list of with names and phone numbers of subcommittee members, cochairs, and the Exec. Director and encourage participants to call anyone if they would like to raise other issues or discuss the work of the Task Force. 

5. Beginning the Discussion: All participants should introduce themselves with a brief self-description. You can help set an informal tone by introducing yourself first, without much fanfare. Where possible, provide participants in advance with a list of others in their group to avoid grouping together attorneys who may have professional or personal contacts that might restrain their candor. Examples of these contacts include opposing counsel or a partner and an associate from the same firm. 

6. Recording the Discussion: A written report will be prepared of the focus group, so it is extremely helpful to have a contemporaneous record of the discussion. The facilitator should arrange to have a notetaker in attendance. It may be easiest to tape record the session, as long as participants agree. The notetaker will take notes, to run the tape, and deal with any technical problems that might arise during the session. Following the session, the notetaker should promptly prepare a report (reviewed and supplemented by the facilitator) -- identifying only the participants' gender/race/ethnicity and position, where relevant. NO NAMES ARE TO BE USED, either of participants or of others they may mention. Completed reports should be circulated to other Subcommittee members, to your Co-Chair liaison, and to Katia Garrett.

IV. Conclusion This memo sets out general guidelines for these informal focus groups. Develop a list of issues that you would like to address (no more than one page) in each group, and distribute it to the group. If you have any questions about running focus groups call Katia Garrett (273-0477) or Vernetta Young (202/806-5325).

TO: Judges, United States Court of Appeals Judges, United States District Court United States Magistrate Judges 

Bankruptcy Judge Teel

The Circuit Judicial Council has appointed a Task Force to assess the impact of gender, race and ethnic background on both judicial proceedings and administrative operations in the District of Columbia Circuit. The study will include examining caserelated proceedings as well as employment practices. The work of the Task Force is being carried out by two special committees: 

Special Committee on Gender and the Special Committee on Race and 

Ethnicity. the 

The Employee Worklife Subcommittee of the Special Committee on 

Gender is studying workplace issues within the Courthouse. In order to identify issues that are of common concern to employees, the Subcommittee is holding initial, confidential "focus groups," or "listening sessions," in small groups. 

The attached memorandum announces the meeting dates of the focus groups and encourages staff participation. The memorandum also explains how to sign up for the sessions and whom to contact if there are any questions. 

As you may be aware, the United States Judicial Conference has recently passed two resolutions on this subject. One endorses encouraging each circuit to sponsor educational programs on how to be sensitive to concerns of bias based on race, ethnicity, gender, age and disability. The second endorses the merits of the circuit judicial councils conducting studies with respect to gender bias in their respective circuits. 

The Task Force and the Circuit Judicial Council are committed to producing meaningful and responsive report and recommendations. We are fortunate to have an exceptionally talented and dedicated staff whose active and candid participation in these focus groups and in other aspects of the study is crucial to the success of the Task Force's work.

# W TH Ind -2- For these reasons, we request that you encourage members of your staff to participate in the focus group sessions. We believe that, in order for this project to be a success, in addition to endorsing the concept of the study, judicial officers must also encourage active participation in the study by members of our staff.

John Garrett Penn Chief Judge United States District Court
