From: Joyce Hens Green, United States District Judge, Task Force Chair Re: Employee Focus Groups and Listening Sessions Date: March 17, 1993 

As you know, the Judicial Council has appointed a Task Force to determine the impact of gender, race, and ethnic background on proceedings in and operations of the courts in the D.C. Circuit. The study is wide-ranging, looking at the litigation processes of the courts as well as the employment practices. The work of the Task Force is being carried out by two Special Committees: the Special Committee on Gender and the 

Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity, chaired and staffed by professors and practicing attorneys. Their work is coordinated and facilitated by Katia Garrett, the 

Executive Director, who works in the Courthouse. The remaining Special Committee chairs and members work outside the courts. 

The Employee Worklife Subcommittee of the Special Committee on Gender is studying workplace issues within the Courthouse. The Subcommittee plans to hold initial, confidential "focus groups" or "listening sessions with small groups of court employees, to assist in identifying issues that are of concern to employees. As Chair of the Task Force, I urge you to encourage all interested employees to participate. 

The attached memorandum announces the focus groups and identifies several dates that are available for several initial groups of employees within the courts. It also explains how to sign up, and whom to contact if there are any questions. 

The D.C. Circuit is one of the first federal courts in the country to study these issues, and we are committed to producing a meaningful and responsive report and recommendations. We are fortunate to have an exceptionally talented and dedicated group of employees in the Court family, and their active and candid participation is important to the success of the Task Force's work. 

If you have any questions, please give me a call.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX L INTERVIEWS WITH JUDGES

INTERVIEW PROTOCOL - JUDGES' INTERVIEWS 

Individual interviews are being sought with each of the judges in the D.C. Circuit federal courts to learn about their observations and experiences in court with issues of race, gender, and ethnicity. The experience of the CJRA Advisory Group last year in interviewing each of the District Court Judges was enormously beneficial to the Group's work. We are particularly eager for input from each of the Judges on their observations and to obtain their suggestions for the direction of the Task Force's work. The format for the interviews is described below: 1. 

Identity of interviewers:

Each interview bas two parts: one on gender issues, one on race and ethnicity issues. 

The purpose of separating the issues is to facilitate deeper exploration of each. Each part will be conducted by a members of the respective Special Committee (Gender - 

Committee co-chairs Prof. Vicki Jackson and Prof. Susan Deller Ross, and 

Committee member Stephen Pollak, Esq.; Race & Ethnicity Committee co-chairs 

Prof. Todd D. Peterson, Vincent H. Cohen, Esq., Vanessa Ruiz, Esq., Joseph Sellers, Esq.) Katia Garrett, the Task Force Executive Director, will be the notetaker at each interview. This format is modeled on that used by the CJRA Advisory Group in its interviews with the District Court judges. 2. 

Scheduling the interview: 3. 

Each judge will be contacted by Katia Garrett to determine the judge's interest and willingness to participate in the interview, and to schedule a convenient time for the interview with interested judges.

If an interview is arranged, a follow-up memo from Katia Garrett will confirm the date and time of the interview and the names of the interviewers. A list of interview topics/questions will be provided at this time. 

Length of interview.

In order not to impose too great a burden on the judge's schedule, a one-hour block of time for the interview will be requested, to be split equally between the Gender Committee and the Race & Ethnicity Committee, as described above. Of course, each judge would be able to set the length of the actual block of time for the interview. The interviewers would welcome additional time to permit fuller exploration of the issues. 1

4. 

List of questions: 

Lists of questions tailored to the different circumstances of appellate and trial-level judges will be prepared, drawn from the lists of questions that were circulated among the Task Force judges. While the lists of the Gender and Race & Ethnicity 

Committees contain overlapping questions, the interviewers are coordinating the questions asked at each interview to avoid duplication. As noted, the lists will be provided in advance to give each judge the opportunity to consider his or her responses. The questions are intended to be a framework for the interview, and the topics chosen are those we felt would benefit from the give and take of conversation. 

The lists are not intended to constrain judges from raising other topics during the interview. 5. 

Written record of the interview: 

To preserve confidentiality and confidence in the interview process, the following procedures will be observed: (a) Katia Garrett will serve as notetaker for all interviews. After each interview, she will prepare a written summary that does not identify the judge by name. For purposes of internal controls, a code will be assigned to each judge. The code list will be kept separately from the summaries. (b) Katia will provide the first draft of the written summary to the interviewer(s), who will review it for accuracy and completeness. After incorporating their changes, Katia will hand-carry a copy of the draft summary to the judge who was interviewed. The judge can then review the summary for accuracy and completeness, and Katia will make any changes needed. (c) A final copy of the written summary will be given to the judge and to the interviewers (listed in item 1). This summary will be provided only to the judge and the interviewers. (d) A report will be prepared based on all the interview summaries, reporting information in general terms, without attribution to or identification of a specific judge (e.g., "All judges but one indicated they are aware of the EEO procedures that are in place for courthouse employees.") This report will be circulated within the Special Committees, to be considered and used with other fact-finding information and materials in preparing subcommittee and 

Committee reports. 2

INTERVIEW OUTLINE - DISTRICT COURT JUDOES

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY 

[**An asterisk denotes questions to be asked by one, but not both, Special Committees in each Judge's interview.]

As a judge, you observe many different courtroom interactions among members of different races and ethnic groups. Have you ever observed individuals being treated differently because of their race or ethnicity by attorneys, jurors, law clerks, marshals, courtroom clerks, or courthouse employees? a) For example, does the race or ethnicity of a defendant have an effect in criminal cases before a jury?

Ver b) 2 2. 3. *4. (၁ Does it have an effect on how the case is treated by the U.S. Attorney's Office? 

Does the race or ethnicity of a party have an effect in civil litigation? If it does, is it related to the type of case (e.g., personal injury, discrimination, commercial)? Have you ever heard racial or ethnic stereotypes being used in court by lawyers, witnesses, jurors, or court personnel? Do you think a judge should take any action if stereotyping language is used in court? e) Do you think race or ethnicity plays a role in the selection of juries? If so, in civil cases, criminal cases, or both? How frequent is it? How frequently is an objection made on this ground?

What is your experience in court with people who speak a language other than English? a) Have you ever observed individuals being treated differently in court because they did not speak English, or because they spoke English with an accent? b) Do you believe that the court adequately provides for individuals who speak little or no English? c) Are prospective jurors treated differently if they are bilingual (speaking English as well as another language), especially if a witness will be testifying in the language in which the prospective juror is fluent? d) Do you have any suggestions for how the system could improve the way it deals with those who are not proficient in English?

Do you believe your own race or ethnicity has affected how you are treated as a judge? 

Are you aware of any special equal employment concerns that courthouse employees may have? 1

*5. 

Are you aware of the process by which EEO claims are resolved in the courthouse? Do you 6. 7. 8. 9. 10. 11. know if any employees have ever expressed any concerns about this process? How well do you think this process works? Do you have any suggestions for changes?

At the public hearing the Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity held in May, several groups indicated that members of certain ethnic minorities have experienced problems in court because some cultural characteristics (for example, hesitating before answering) are misinterpreted. 

Have you ever observed such instances in court? Is there anything that the court can or should do about such problems?

The court makes appointments of attorneys to special committees to assist the court, or as special masters or experts, and it selects individuals to attend the judicial conference. Do you think that it is desirable as a goal, over time, to appoint significant numbers of racial and ethnic minorities, or do you believe that race and ethnicity should not be considered in the appointment process? In your own recommendations or appointments of attorneys for such positions, what role, if any, does race or ethnicity play?

Choice of a law clerk is a very personal decision for each judge, and we understand that a range of factors may play a role in selection decisions. Understanding this, do you think that it is desirable to have as a goal that, over time, you would have significant numbers of racial and ethnic minorities as law clerks? Do you believe it would be appropriate to consider racial and ethnic diversity in the clerkship selection process? Do you think that your own race or ethnicity affects the perception of how you handle cases?

During our public hearing, we hear from an African-American woman who stated that it was difficult for minority pro se litigants to obtain legal help in bringing discrimination cases. Do you think that the system adequately provides for legal assistance in discrimination cases? 

Do you have any suggestions for issues that the Task Force should explore in the area of race and ethnicity? 2

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY 

Please note that these questions are designed for use in oral interviews. If you feel that a question is clear, please feel free to rephrase the question or to clarify it in your response. 2. 3. 5. 

1. We recognize that as an appellate judge you do not have the opportunity to see parties and witnesses that trial-level judges have. Understanding this, have you ever sensed whether through review of briefs, records, or in oral argument that the race or ethnicity of either a party or a witness affected how that party or witness was perceived or treated by lawyers, judges or jurors in trial? If so, please describe how. (a) Is there any other aspect of the trial-level criminal or civil judicial process that you have reviewed in which you have sensed that race or ethnicity may have had an effect? 

For example, have you sensed this in the treatment of cases by the U.S. Attorney's 

Office, the selection of juries, the assessment of credibility, or in the evaluation of personal injuries? If so, please describe.

As a judge, you observe courtroom interactions among a range of attorneys, some of whom are minority and some non-minority. Have you perceived any differences in the advocacy styles of minority and non-minority attorneys? If so, what have you perceived?

Do you believe your own race or ethnicity has affected how you are treated as a judge, either positively or negatively? In general, have you perceived attorneys acting differently depending on the race or ethnicity of the judges on a panel? For example, have you ever observed attorneys try to appeal to the race or ethnicity of a judge? If so, what have you perceived? 

4. Do you think that your own race or ethnicity affects the public perception, among lawyers and non-lawyers, of how you handle cases, or do you think race or ethnicity is not a factor in their perception? Please explain.

Can you give a rough assessment of what the breakdown is by race and ethnicity of the attorneys who have appeared before you? If there is a minority advocate on one side, do you think, based on your experience in the D.C. Circuit, that opposing counsel act differently in the courtroom, or have you observed no differences? If you have observed differences, what are they? 

6. Have you ever had a case on appeal where the record included testimony provided through an interpreter for a witness who did not speak English or spoke only limited English? If so, does the fact that testimony has been translated make any difference to you for example, in how you review the record? 7. Have you ever encountered any inadequacies in the record, relating to translated testimony? 

If so, how have you handled them? Have you ever had an occasion where the issue of the adequacy of interpretation or translation of testimony was presented on appeal? If so, what was the issue presented? 

What experience do you have in court with people who speak accented English? Have you ever observed individuals being treated differently in court because they spoke English with an accent? Please describe your experiences, as well as any differences you may have observed. 1

Are you aware of what provisions exist for language minorities in positions that have contact with the public, such as the Clerk's Office, the Marshals Service, and the Court Security 

Officers? Do you think these provisions are adequate? Do you have any sense of whether language minorities, individuals with limited or no English, are sufficiently able to find locations they may need in the Courthouse? Please explain. .. 10. What kind of contact do you have with the various non-judicial courthouse personnel, apart from chambers staff? Are you satisfied that the non-judicial workforce fully utilizes minority employees? 11. 12. 13. 14. 15. 16. 17. 

Are you familiar with the court's EEO processes? Do you know if any employee has ever expressed any concerns about this process? How well do you think the process works? Do you have any suggestions for changes?

Are you aware of any equal employment concerns that any courthouse employee or employees may have?

The Court makes appointments of attorneys to committees to assist the Court, or as special masters, and it selects individuals to attend the Judicial Conference. Do you think that it is desirable to have as a goal, over time, that a significant number of racial and ethnic minority attorneys will be appointed? Or do you believe that race or ethnicity should not be considered in the appointment or invitation process? In your own recommendations, appointments, or invitations of attorneys, what role, if any, does race or ethnicity play?

The choice of a law clerk is a very personal decision for each judge, and we understand that a range of factors may play a role in selection decisions. Understanding this, do you think that it is desirable to have as a goal that, over time, you would have significant numbers of racial and ethnic minorities as law clerks? Or do you believe that race or ethnicity should not be considered in the selection process? Please explain. (a) Do you have the sense that there are sufficient qualified minority applicants in the pool of applications you receive? Are there any measures that you think could be undertaken to increase the pool of qualified minority applicants?

During our public hearing, we heard from an African American woman who stated that it was difficult for minority pro se litigants to obtain legal help in bringing cases. Are you familiar with the handling of pro se matters in the D.C. Circuit? Do you think that the system adequately provides for legal assistance? Please explain.

Based on your experience within the judicial system, and mindful of any restrictions there are on judicial conduct, do you think that there are any measures the Court might take to enhance its visibility in or familiarity to minority communities? To what extent do you think that this is an appropriate consideration for the Court? Please explain.

Finally, what changes do you see over the time you have been on the bench (or in practice) with respect to issues of race and ethnicity in the federal courts? What do you think the future holds? Do you have any suggestions for issues that the Task Force should explore in the area of race and ethnicity? 2

Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity of the D.C. Circuit Task 

Force on Gender, Race and Ethnic Bias will be held on Tuesday, May 

11, 1993, from 8:30 a.m. until 6:30 p.m. in the Thurgood Marshall

Federal Judiciary Building (Lower Level), One Columbus Circle, NE. 

Preliminary areas identified for study include:

**Criminal Justice including bail, charging, and sentencing

**Class, Race & Gender **Litigation Process including unique issues confronting minority women as lawyers or parties

**Federal Equality Law including court's attitudes toward cases in which rainorities and women are fraquently parties

TASK FORCE OF THE DISTRICT OF COLUMBIA CIRCUIT Dean ON GENDER, RACE AND ETHNIC BIAS United States Courthouse Washington, D.C. 20001 

202/273-0477 PAX: 202/273-0559 

NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING Law School Washington, DC 20008 

Dear Dean.

The Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity of the D.C. Circuit Task Force is charged by the D.C. Circuit Judicial Council with the responsibility of determining the impact of race and ethnicity on proceedings in and operations of the federal trial and appellate courts in the D.C. Circuit. The Task Force will make specific recommendations to address any areas of concern that may be identified. The enclosed news release describes the make-up of the Task Force and its mandate. 

On May 11, 1993, from 8:30 am to 6:30 p.m., the Special Committee on Race and

Ethnicity is holding a public bearing at the Thurgood Marshall Federal Judiciary Building to identify the range of issues of greatest concern to individuals, organizations, and others who use or are affected by the D.C. federal courts. The results of this hearing will guide and inform the Special Committee's work. 

The enclosed flyer, registration form, and instructions provide more detail about the hearing and the preliminary areas the Committee has identified for exploration. This study is among the first in any federal court, and we are dedicated to conducting a thorough and impartial exploration of the issues. We will welcome comments and testimony from all quarters.

If you have any questions, or would like to learn more about the work of the Special 

Committee and the Task Force, please contact Katherine L. Garrett, Executive Director of the Task Force at the above number.

NOTICE OF PUBLIC HEARING Pres. 

Bar Assn Washington, DC 20001 

Dear!

The Special Committee on Race and Ethnicity of the D.C. Circuit Task Force is charged by the D.C. Circuit Judicial Council with the responsibility of determining the impact of race and ethnicity on proceedings in and operations of the federal trial and appellate courts in the D.C. Circuit. The Task Force will make specific recommendations to address any areas of concern that may be identified. The enclosed news release describes the make-up of the Task Force and its mandate. 

On May 11, 1993, from 8:30 am. to 6:30 p.m., the Special Committee on Race and

Ethnicity is holding a public hearing at the Federal Judiciary Building to identify the range of issues of greatest concern to this community through comments from affected individuals, organizations, and others who use the D.C. federal courts. We hope you or a representative can attend or provide written testimony.

The preliminary areas the Committee has identified for study are: 

Criminal Justice including bail, charging, and sentencing decisions Access to Justice - including availability of attorneys to minority populations, and court access issues for non-English speakers Class, Race & Gender including unique issues confronting minority women as lawyers or parties Litigation Process including the role race or ethnicity may play in substantive outcomes, as well as in professional interactions among attorneys, judges, litigants, witnesses and jurors Employee Worklife including issues affecting functions and operations of courthouse staff 50 2

The purpose of the public hearing is to help the Committee identify additional specific issues and areas of inquiry and to clarify which issues are of greatest concern. 

The potential scope of our work is quite broad, as to understand the effect of race and ethnicity on the courts we will need to understand how race and ethnicity affect the legal profession, the judiciary, federal court litigants, and federal court employees. 

This study is the first of its kind in any federal court, and we are dedicated to conducting a thorough and impartial exploration of the issues. Your input at this preliminary stage of our work would be invaluable, and we urge that you attend the hearing. 

If you or a representative cannot attend the public bearing, or would prefer not to testify, please send us your written comments or testimony to the address above. The Committee can also arrange for confidential testimony or interviews, and will treat all information received with the utmost confidentiality. SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY 

OF THE D.C. CIRCUIT TASK FORCE ON GENDER, RACE, AND ETHNIC BLAS GUIDELINES FOR TESTIMONY Public Hearing 

The Special Committee is looking at the federal courts in the D.C. Circuit: the U.S. 

District Court for the District of Columbia and the U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia. It is not looking at the local courts of the District of 

Columbia (Superior Court, D.C. Court of Appeals), or at any other federal court that happens to be in D.C. (such as the Claims Court or Tax Court). 

The Special Committee cannot decide individual cases, and cannot help you with your legal case.

While the Committee cannot give legal advice or become involved in any legal case, it would like to know if you thought you were treated unfairly. The Committee can learn from your experiences and help the D.C. Circuit prevent others from being treated unfairly.

The Special Committee is exploring procedural issues: are individuals treated with dignity and courtesy by court participants regardless of race/ethnicity? 

The Special Committee is also concerned with substantive issues: is decision-making fair and impartial on substantive matters? Do neutral practices nonetheless have significant racial/ethnic biased results? Written materials will help the Special Committee - let the Committee know if there is a transcript, written decision, or report that relates to the matter you are testifying about. Submit any written materials to the address below NO LATER THAN 

FRIDAY, MAY 14, 1993. 

The Committee is interested in hearing from as many concerned individuals and organizations as possible. To do so, the Committee is carefully scheduling the time for each person, to permit as many people as possible explain their concerns to the Committee. The Committee will try to accommodate as many people as possible, but it may not be able to hear from everyone today. The Committee encourages all participants to submit any additional concerns or information in writing, NO LATER 

THAN FRIDAY, MAY 14, 1993.

SPECIAL COMMITTEE ON RACE AND ETHNICITY APPENDIX N ACCESS TO JUSTICE MATERIALS

August 8, 1994 202/273-0477 FAX: 202/273-0669 

Dear [Legal Service Provider] On behalf of the Special Committee on Race & Ethnicity of the  

Gender, Race & Ethnic Bias, we are contacting you for your assistance in studying and understanding whether, and to what extent, race or ethnicity plays a role in issues of access to the federal courts of the D.C. Circuit. We understand that your organization provides pro bono legal services to people in the D.C. community, and that these services include matters that may lead to federal court litigation. is one of a number of organizations providing such services that we are contacting as part of our effort to better understand the numbers and kinds of potential federal court matters for which legal assistance is sought, as well as the demographics of the people seeking such assistance. The Task Force was charged by the D.C. Circuit Judicial Council with the task of studying whether, and to what extent, gender, race, or ethnicity play a role in the administration and proceedings of the D.C. Circuit courts the District Court, the Court of Appeals, and the 

Bankruptcy Court. The work of the Special Committee on Race & Ethnicity began in May 1993 with a public hearing, at which several witnesses testified to specific concerns relating to access to legal representation and the courts. A general description of the Task Force and its two Special 

Committees is enclosed to provide you some idea of the nature of this project. 

In order to provide a context for research we have been conducting on issues of access and representation in federal court, we are contacting legal services providers such as you for assistance. 

Specifically, it would be helpful if you could provide by September 1, 1994, the following information: the number and percentage of the people contacting your organization for assistance who present matters that are potential federal court claims the number and percentage of these matters you are able to handle (either in-house or through referral to other organizations or practitioners) the demographics (gender, race, ethnicity, etc.) of the individuals contacting your organization for assistance on these matters any guidelines or qualifications you apply in determining to provide assistance For the first three items, if you are able to provide the information, please also let us know the basis of your assessment (e.g., records, logs, personal experience). 

Finally, we are very interested in hearing your own observations and experiences in the provision of legal services on potential federal court matters, and your own assessment of whether

and to what extent if at all, race or ethnicity may play a role in this area. To this end, we welcome any additional comments or information you may have. If you would be interested in participating in a discussion group on this topic during the week of September 5, 1994, please call Katherine 

Garrett (202/273-0477), the Executive Director of the Task Force.

Thank you for your attention to this matter. If you have any questions about the Task Force project, or about this aspect of our work, please feel free to call: Claudia Withers (202/463-7088) or Shanlon Wu (202/514-8203), members of the Access to Justice Subcommittee, or Katherine Garrett.
